ITNSSS31

Proto-question Stage 1

Will the U.S. Congress pass federal legislation mandating universal DNA synthesis screening (covering non-federally-funded orders) by December 31, 2026?

Why this question? The paper discusses AI-enabled pathogen design as a key risk pathway and notes that DNA synthesis screening is a critical mitigation. The current OSTP Framework only covers federally funded research. The Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 (S. 3741), introduced by Senators Cotton (R-AR) and Klobuchar (D-MN) on January 29, 2026, would extend screening requirements to all orders. Whether Congress passes this or similar legislation is genuinely uncertain — it has bipartisan support but Congressional timelines are unpredictable.

Paper reference: The paper notes that 'improved real-time information sharing and screening of dual-use materials may increase the economic and political costs of bioweapon development' (Section 3, bullet on offense-defense balance). Table 9 references scenarios where AI helps design pathogens that bypass detection. The paper also discusses DNA synthesis screening evasion (citing Wittmann et al. 2025).

Background

Nucleic acid synthesis screening is a biosecurity measure in which providers of synthetic DNA and RNA check incoming orders against databases of known pathogen sequences to prevent malicious actors from obtaining dangerous biological materials. The current U.S. screening regime is governed by the "Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening," released by the White House Office of Science and Technology Policy (OSTP) in September 2024 (https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf). This Framework defines key terms including "Sequence of Concern" (broadly, a nucleotide sequence that is a best match to a sequence from a federally regulated pathogen or toxin) and establishes requirements for sequence-level and customer-level screening. Critically, the 2024 OSTP Framework applies only as a condition of receiving U.S. governmental life sciences research funding — it does not mandate screening for privately funded or commercial orders. On May 5, 2025, President Trump signed Executive Order 14292, "Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research" (https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/05/08/2025-08266/improving-the-safety-and-security-of-biological-research), which directed OSTP to develop a strategy that "shall include actions to achieve comprehensive, scalable, and verifiable nucleic acid synthesis screening in non-federally funded" contexts. However, this executive order does not itself create a legal mandate for universal screening — it only directs the development of a strategy. On January 29, 2026, Senators Tom Cotton (R-AR) and Amy Klobuchar (D-MN) introduced S. 3741, the "Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026" (https://www.congress.gov/bill/119th-congress/senate-bill/3741/all-info). The bill would require the Secretary of Commerce to promulgate regulations requiring all nucleic acid synthesis providers to screen orders — not just those involving federal funding. The bill was read twice and referred to the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science, and Transportation on January 29, 2026. As of March 19, 2026, no further committee action (hearing, markup, or vote) has been reported. The bill has bipartisan sponsorship and endorsements from organizations including the Nuclear Threat Initiative (NTI), the Federation of American Scientists (FAS), and the Johns Hopkins Center for Health Security. No companion bill has been identified in the House of Representatives. The FY2026 National Defense Authorization Act (NDAA), signed into law on December 18, 2025, included the BIOSECURE Act (restricting federal procurement from certain biotechnology companies of concern) but did not include a universal DNA synthesis screening mandate. Historically, most bills introduced in Congress do not advance beyond committee referral. However, S. 3741 has notable bipartisan support, alignment with the executive branch's stated goals in EO 14292, and endorsements from prominent biosecurity organizations. The 119th Congress (2025–2026) expires in January 2027. ---

Resolution criteria

This question resolves Yes if, between March 19, 2026 (00:00 UTC) and December 31, 2026 (23:59 UTC), the President of the United States signs into law any federal legislation that includes a legally binding requirement for nucleic acid synthesis providers operating in the United States to screen synthesis orders against sequences of concern, where this requirement applies to all orders — not solely those funded by U.S. government research funding. Specific clarifications: 1. Definition of "pass": The legislation must be signed into law by the President, or enacted through a congressional override of a presidential veto. Mere passage by one or both chambers of Congress does not suffice. 2. Definition of "DNA synthesis screening": For purposes of this question, "nucleic acid synthesis screening" refers to the practice of checking orders for synthetic nucleic acids (DNA or RNA) against databases of sequences associated with pathogens, toxins, or other biological agents of concern, and conducting follow-up screening (including customer verification) when a potential match is identified, consistent with the definition in the 2024 OSTP Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening (https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf). 3. "Universal" requirement: The mandate must apply to all commercial nucleic acid synthesis providers operating in the United States (i.e., it is not limited to providers serving federally funded customers or to federally funded orders only). It is sufficient if the law requires all providers to screen all orders; it is not necessary that the law separately mandate obligations on purchasers. 4. Broader legislation: The requirement need not be enacted as a standalone bill. If the relevant DNA synthesis screening mandate is included as a provision within a broader bill (e.g., an omnibus spending bill, a National Defense Authorization Act, or any other legislative vehicle), that satisfies the resolution criteria, provided the signed law contains a legally binding universal screening requirement as defined above. 5. Regulatory delegation: If the signed legislation directs a federal agency (e.g., the Department of Commerce) to promulgate regulations establishing universal screening, this counts — even if the implementing regulations have not yet been finalized by December 31, 2026 — as long as the statute itself creates a binding legal mandate for universal screening (not merely a directive to "study" or "develop a strategy" for screening).

Forecast rationale

This question asks whether the U.S. President will sign into law legislation mandating universal nucleic acid synthesis screening (covering all orders, not just federally funded ones) by December 31, 2026. Here is my analysis: Current State: S. 3741, introduced January 29, 2026, by Senators Cotton and Klobuchar, is the primary legislative vehicle. As of March 19, 2026, it has been referred to the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science, and Transportation with no hearings, markups, or further action reported [3539a3]. Crucially, no House companion bill exists. Base Rates Against: Historical base rates are strongly unfavorable. Only 1-4% of introduced bills become law. ~80-90% of bills referred to committee never receive a hearing. Bills introduced in the second year of a Congress in an election year face even worse odds. The absence of a House companion is a major structural bottleneck. Favorable Factors: Bipartisan sponsorship (Cotton-Klobuchar), alignment with EO 14292, institutional endorsements from NTI, FAS, and Johns Hopkins, industry support from major players wanting a level playing field [a04311], and the BIOSECURE Act precedent showing biosecurity provisions can ride must-pass bills like the NDAA [2a07a1]. Critical Negative Signal: The FY2026 NDAA (signed December 18, 2025) included the BIOSECURE Act but did NOT include a universal screening mandate, even though the concept was already being discussed. This suggests it was not considered a 'must-pass' priority for that cycle. Most Plausible Pathway: Attachment to the FY2027 NDAA or year-end omnibus. However, this requires: (1) committee action on S. 3741 or equivalent language being drafted, (2) a House process or companion, (3) surviving conference negotiations, and (4) presidential signature. The election year compresses the legislative calendar significantly. Market Signal: Manifold Markets reportedly estimates ~40% for 'Will the U.S. mandate DNA synthesis screening for all providers by 2027?' This is essentially the same timeframe. However, I believe this estimate is too high given the concrete structural obstacles—no committee action after nearly two months, no House companion, election year dynamics, and the precedent of the FY2026 NDAA excluding this provision. Key Uncertainties: 1. Will S. 3741 receive committee action? If hearings/markup occur by summer 2026, probability increases significantly (perhaps to 20-25%). If it remains stalled through September, probability drops to ~3-5%. 2. Will a House companion bill emerge? Without one, the bill must be attached to a larger vehicle. The emergence of a House companion would be a strong positive signal. 3. Will the NDAA or omnibus serve as a vehicle? If leadership prioritizes this for the FY2027 NDAA, the probability rises substantially. If it's excluded (as it was from FY2026 NDAA), standalone passage is extremely unlikely. 4. Will an external trigger (biosecurity incident, AI-bio threat) create urgency? A catalyzing event could dramatically accelerate the timeline. Synthesis: Blending the low base rates (~2-5%), the favorable but insufficient political dynamics, the concrete structural obstacles, and partially weighting the ~40% Manifold estimate (which I believe overestimates the probability given current legislative stagnation), I arrive at approximately 10%.

Adversarial review PASS

Assessment: PASS

ASSESSMENT: PASS REVIEW: This is a well-constructed forecasting question with accurate background facts, clear resolution criteria, and meaningful uncertainty. Key findings from research: 1. Background facts are accurate: S. 3741 was indeed introduced on January 29, 2026, by Senators Cotton and Klobuchar, referred to the Senate Commerce Committee, and has had no further committee action as of March 19, 2026. The description of EO 14292, the 2024 OSTP Framework, and the FY2026 NDAA's BIOSECURE Act (without universal screening) are all verified. 2. Resolution criteria are well-specified: The question properly handles key edge cases — regulatory delegation (point 5), inclusion in broader legislation (point 4), and the distinction between a mandate and a mere "study" directive. These are the exact edge cases that would matter for this topic. 3. The question captures genuine uncertainty: While the base rate for bills dying in committee is high (~95%), there are plausible paths to YES: (a) inclusion as a provision in the FY2027 NDAA (the FY2026 NDAA was signed Dec 18, 2025, so FY2027 NDAA could be signed in Dec 2026); (b) attachment to omnibus spending legislation; (c) accelerated standalone passage due to bipartisan sponsorship and alignment with EO 14292. The probability is likely low (5-15%) but not trivially zero, making it informative for forecasters. 4. One nuance worth noting but not disqualifying: The FAS analysis [796bf1] describes S. 3741 as giving the Administration 90 days to assess and design biosecurity oversight, suggesting it may be more of a framework-directive than a direct operational mandate. However, the bill's own summary (from congress.gov and press releases) states it would "require the Secretary of Commerce to promulgate regulations requiring all nucleic acid synthesis providers to screen orders," and the resolution criteria explicitly address this scenario in point #5 (regulatory delegation counts if the statute creates a binding mandate). 5. No companion House bill is correctly noted. H.R. 3029 (Nucleic Acid Standards for Biosecurity Act) is a related but distinct bill focused on NIST developing standards rather than mandating universal screening. HR 4729 exists to codify EO 14292 but that EO itself only directs strategy development, not a binding mandate. 6. The time horizon (March-December 2026) is appropriate for the legislative dynamics involved — tight enough to be interesting but allowing for the NDAA or omnibus vehicle in late 2026. Initial: 18%→Research-informed: 4%
SQ1: What is the current status and realistic legislative trajectory of S. 3741 (the Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026) in the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science, and Transportation?
As of March 19, 2026, S. 3741 (the Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026), introduced on January 29, 2026 by Senators Tom Cotton (R-AR) and Amy Klobuchar (D-MN), remains stalled at the earliest stage of the legislative process—referred to the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science, and Transportation with no committee hearing, markup, or vote scheduled. The committee has held multiple markups in 2026 (February 12, March 4, March 12, and one announced for March 25), none of which included S. 3741. The February 12 markup advanced eight bipartisan measures, none related to biosecurity [f736c9]. The March 4 markup focused on NASA reauthorization and NOAA weather legislation, while the March 12 session handled nominations. The March 25 markup agenda includes Coast Guard promotions and other items, but not S. 3741. Chairman Ted Cruz (R-TX) has made no identifiable public statement regarding S. 3741 or nucleic acid synthesis screening; his publicly stated 2026 priorities center on NASA/space, aviation safety, AI regulation (the SANDBOX Act), weather forecasting modernization, and Section 230 reform. The committee's legislative pipeline appears heavily focused on these areas. The Senate's 2026 legislative calendar shows significant recess periods (March 30–April 10, May breaks, June–July recess, August 10–September 11, October 5–November 6, and December 21–31), compressing available legislative time. Despite strong bipartisan sponsorship and endorsements from organizations like FAS [3cb56b], NTI, and Johns Hopkins Center for Health Security, the absence of any committee action nearly two months after introduction—combined with Chairman Cruz's silence on the bill and the committee's focus on other priorities—signals that S. 3741 faces significant hurdles to advancing through the committee in the near term.
SQ2: Does a companion bill to S. 3741 exist in the U.S. House of Representatives, or is there an active effort to introduce one or attach universal DNA synthesis screening provisions to House legislation?
As of March 19, 2026, no companion bill to S. 3741 (the Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026) exists in the U.S. House of Representatives. S. 3741, introduced on January 29, 2026 by Senators Tom Cotton (R-AR) and Amy Klobuchar (D-MN), mandates that all nucleic acid synthesis providers—not just those handling federally funded orders—implement screening protocols [cb8954]. It was referred to the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science, and Transportation but has not yet been advanced by that committee [fd053f]. The only House bill addressing DNA synthesis screening is H.R. 3029 (the Nucleic Acid Standards for Biosecurity Act), introduced April 28, 2025, which focuses on directing NIST to develop voluntary, stakeholder-driven standards rather than mandating universal screening by all providers. H.R. 3029 was passed by the House Committee on Science, Space, and Technology around April 29, 2025. The House Committee on Energy and Commerce held a hearing on biosecurity and AI on December 17, 2025 where experts urged mandatory gene synthesis screening, but no legislation has resulted. Representative Julia Brownley (D-CA), who championed the Securing Gene Synthesis Act (H.R. 4702) in the 118th Congress and led a bipartisan letter urging mandatory screening, has not publicly announced plans to introduce a companion to S. 3741 in the 119th Congress. No active efforts to attach universal DNA synthesis screening mandates to must-pass House legislation (such as the NDAA or appropriations bills) have been publicly identified. The FY2026 NDAA, signed into law on December 18, 2025, included the BIOSECURE Act but not DNA synthesis screening mandates. This absence of a House vehicle represents a critical bottleneck for enactment of universal screening legislation.
SQ3: What are the major must-pass legislative vehicles remaining in the 119th Congress (2025-2026) that could serve as carriers for a universal nucleic acid synthesis screening mandate, and what is the realistic timeline for each?
Major Must-Pass Legislative Vehicles and Timelines for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening Mandate (March 19 – December 31, 2026) Several must-pass legislative vehicles remain in the 119th Congress that could theoretically carry a universal nucleic acid synthesis screening mandate before December 31, 2026: 1. FY2027 NDAA (Most Likely Vehicle): The FY2027 NDAA is the single most probable carrier. Historical precedent strongly supports enactment by December 31, 2026: the FY2026 NDAA was signed December 18, 2025; FY2025 on December 23, 2024; FY2024 on December 22, 2023; FY2023 on December 23, 2022; FY2022 on December 27, 2021. The NDAA has been enacted before year-end in most recent years. However, the FY2027 process is running behind schedule — the President's budget request is now expected the week of March 30, 2026, delayed from the statutory first-Monday-in-February deadline. House members' NDAA request deadlines were in February-March 2026. Committee markups typically occur in summer (June-July), with floor votes in fall, and conference/final passage in December. This timeline is tight but feasible for a December 2026 signing. 2. Iran War Defense Supplemental (New, High-Probability Vehicle): As of mid-March 2026, the Pentagon is seeking $50–200+ billion in supplemental funding for the Iran conflict. Speaker Johnson called this "inevitable" (reported March 2026). This must-pass supplemental could move through Congress in 2026 and could serve as a vehicle for biosecurity provisions, though its scope and timeline remain highly uncertain. 3. FY2026 Homeland Security Appropriations (Remaining Bill): 11 of 12 FY2026 appropriations bills have been enacted. Only DHS funding remains outstanding as of March 19, 2026, with a partial DHS shutdown ongoing since mid-February. The House passed H.R. 7744 on March 5, 2026, but the Senate rejected it on March 12, 2026. This bill will eventually need resolution, potentially through a CR or negotiated bill, but as a single-agency bill it is a less likely vehicle for biosecurity provisions. 4. Second Reconciliation Bill ("Reconciliation 2.0"): House Republicans are eyeing a second reconciliation package focused on healthcare, housing, energy, and potentially defense spending. As of March 2026, this is in early conceptual stages. Budget reconciliation uses expedited Senate procedures (no filibuster), but it is constrained to budgetary matters by the Byrd Rule, making it an unlikely vehicle for regulatory mandates like synthesis screening. Timeline for passage, if pursued, is unclear but could extend into late 2026. 5. FY2027 Appropriations: The FY2027 budget process is just beginning. Full-year FY2027 appropriations bills are extremely unlikely to be enacted by December 31, 2026; historically, appropriations are rarely completed on time, and a CR extending FY2026 levels would be the more likely outcome. 6. Standalone Biosecurity Bills: S.3741 (Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026), introduced January 29, 2026, by Senators Cotton and Klobuchar, directly mandates universal nucleic acid synthesis screening through the Commerce Department. H.R. 3029 (Nucleic Acid Standards for Biosecurity Act) is a narrower House bill. Neither has seen committee action as of March 2026. As standalone bills, their chances of floor passage are low, but their provisions could be attached to a must-pass vehicle like the FY2027 NDAA or a supplemental. Congressional Calendar: The 2026 midterm elections are November 3, 2026. The Senate has a State Work Period from October 5 through November 6. Both chambers target adjournment by approximately December 18, 2026. The post-election lame-duck session (approximately November 9 – December 20) is historically when the NDAA and year-end legislative packages are finalized. The 119th Congress expires January 3, 2027. Overall Assessment: The FY2027 NDAA and the Iran war supplemental are the two most viable must-pass vehicles that could carry a universal synthesis screening mandate by December 31, 2026. The NDAA's historical track record of late-December enactment makes it the most realistic carrier, though the delayed FY2027 budget process introduces scheduling risk. The emerging Iran supplemental creates an additional opportunity but is less predictable in timing and scope.
SQ4: What is the Trump administration's current position on mandatory universal nucleic acid synthesis screening legislation, and would the President likely sign such a bill if it reached his desk?
The Trump administration has signaled strong interest in extending nucleic acid synthesis screening beyond federally funded orders through Executive Order 14292 (signed May 5, 2025), which directed OSTP to revise the 2024 Framework within 90 days (~August 3, 2025) and develop a broader strategy—including legislative proposals—within 180 days (~November 1, 2025). However, as of March 2026, there is no public evidence that either the revised framework or the broader strategy has been released, meaning both deadlines appear to have been missed. The administration's AI Action Plan (July 2025) addressed nucleic acid synthesis screening but only proposed requirements tied to federally funded institutions, not universal mandates for all providers [b2247e]. S. 3741, the Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026, introduced January 29, 2026 by Senators Cotton (R-AR) and Klobuchar (D-MN), would mandate universal screening by all synthesis providers regardless of funding source [049632], but the Trump administration has not explicitly endorsed this bill. Multiple endorsements have come from organizations like the National Security Commission on Emerging Biotechnology and NTI, but not the White House. A separate House bill, H.R. 3029 (the Nucleic Acid Standards for Biosecurity Act), takes a lighter voluntary-standards approach. A tension exists between the administration's general deregulatory philosophy and the biosecurity imperative: the EO's language about "comprehensive, scalable, and verifiable" screening in non-federally funded contexts suggests support for extending regulation, but the AI Action Plan's narrower focus on federal funding conditions and the missed deadlines for the broader strategy suggest bureaucratic or philosophical friction in translating that aspiration into concrete action or legislative proposals.
SQ5: What are the key political, industry, and practical obstacles that could prevent universal nucleic acid synthesis screening legislation from being enacted by December 31, 2026?
S. 3741, the Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026, was introduced on January 29, 2026, by Senators Tom Cotton (R-AR) and Amy Klobuchar (D-MN) and referred to the Senate Commerce, Science, and Transportation Committee, where it remains in "Introduced" status as of March 19, 2026 [c82b0c]. The bill would mandate the Secretary of Commerce to require all synthesis providers to screen orders for sequences of concern and verify customer identities [c82b0c]. Multiple obstacles stand between this bill and enactment by December 31, 2026: Industry Stances: Major providers—Twist Bioscience, IDT, GenScript, and Thermo Fisher—all voluntarily screen orders under the IGSC Harmonized Screening Protocol and have submitted self-attestation documents to comply with the Biden-era Framework. Twist Bioscience, through VP James Diggans's December 17, 2025 congressional testimony, has been a vocal proponent of strengthened screening. The ARI's January 2026 paper "Competitive Compliance" argues mandatory uniform standards support innovation and prevent regulatory arbitrage, suggesting some industry actors favor a mandate. However, no public statements from IDT, GenScript, or Thermo Fisher explicitly endorsing or opposing S. 3741's mandatory approach were identified. GenScript faces separate national security scrutiny over its China ties. Base Rate Data: According to GovTrack, only about 2-3% of bills introduced in Congress are enacted into law, and only approximately 11% make it past committee. The BIOSECURE Act—a comparable biosecurity measure—took roughly two full congressional sessions (first introduced in 2023, enacted December 18, 2025) and ultimately passed only by being attached to the must-pass FY2026 NDAA, illustrating that standalone biosecurity bills face steep odds without a legislative vehicle. Trump Administration's Deregulatory Stance vs. Biosecurity: The Trump administration's general posture is strongly deregulatory (10-to-1 deregulation executive order, January 31, 2025). However, Trump's May 5, 2025 Executive Order on "Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research" explicitly calls for a strategy to "achieve comprehensive, scalable, and verifiable nucleic acid synthesis screening in non-federally funded" contexts, signaling some openness to expanded screening. This creates a tension: the administration appears sympathetic to biosecurity goals but may prefer executive/voluntary pathways over congressional mandates on private industry. Competing Legislative Priorities: The 119th Congress's first session was dominated by the "One Big Beautiful Bill" reconciliation act (signed July 4, 2025) and the FY2026 NDAA (signed December 18, 2025). The second session (2026) faces midterm election pressures, government funding battles (FY2027 appropriations and potential continuing resolutions), and implementation oversight of the reconciliation law. The Senate's 2026 calendar includes significant recess periods [010255], and the bill must still receive a committee hearing, markup, floor votes in both chambers, and presidential signature—all within roughly 9 months. Alternative Legislative Approaches: H.R. 3029, the Nucleic Acid Standards for Biosecurity Act (passed House Science Committee April 2025), takes a voluntary, NIST-led standards approach rather than a Commerce Department mandate, representing a competing and less prescriptive vision. This alternative could dilute momentum for mandatory screening or serve as a compromise vehicle. Key Pathway: The most plausible route to enactment would be attachment to a must-pass vehicle like the FY2027 NDAA (typically finalized in late 2026), mirroring the BIOSECURE Act's path. However, this would require the bill's provisions to gain sufficient bipartisan momentum and committee advancement in a compressed timeline.

Proto-question Stage 1

Will the BWC Working Group on Strengthening the Convention produce a consensus final report recommending a formal verification or compliance mechanism by December 31, 2026?

Why this question? The paper emphasizes that the BWC 'does not have a formal verification process' as a major gap enabling covert state bioweapons programs. The BWC Working Group has its 8th session (Feb 2026) and 9th session (Aug 17-21, 2026) scheduled. Whether it achieves consensus on verification is genuinely uncertain given geopolitical tensions between Russia, China, and Western states. This is a critical institutional indicator of whether the international community can constrain the state-level risks the paper models.

Paper reference: The paper notes that '184 states are party to the BWC' but that 'the BWC does not have a formal verification process and no state has publicly acknowledged a current offensive bioweapon programme' (Section 1). Section 5 discusses attribution as a key deterrent and notes the 'absence of verification mechanisms within the BWC.'

Background

The Biological Weapons Convention (BWC; full title: Convention on the Prohibition of the Development, Production and Stockpiling of Bacteriological (Biological) and Toxin Weapons and on their Destruction) is a multilateral disarmament treaty banning biological weapons. As of 2025, the BWC has 189 States Parties and 4 Signatory States (Egypt, Haiti, Somalia, and Syria). Unlike the Chemical Weapons Convention (CWC), which is overseen by the Organisation for the Prohibition of Chemical Weapons (OPCW) and includes a comprehensive verification regime with routine inspections, the BWC does not have a formal verification process or a standing organizational body dedicated to monitoring compliance. This absence has been widely identified as a major gap in the international biosecurity regime. The Ninth Review Conference of the BWC (November–December 2022) established a Working Group on the Strengthening of the Convention (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_Weapons_Convention). Under paragraph 16, Part II of the Final Document (BWC/CONF.IX/9, available at https://docs.un.org/en/BWC/CONF.IX/9), the Working Group is mandated to "identify, examine and develop specific and effective measures, including possible legally-binding measures, and to make recommendations to strengthen and institutionalize the Convention in all its aspects." The Working Group is tasked with submitting a final report including "conclusions and recommendations" to the Tenth Review Conference (scheduled for 2027), or earlier at a Special Conference or Meeting of States Parties. The Working Group operates under the Rules of Procedure of the Ninth Review Conference (BWC/CONF.IX/2, available at https://docs.un.org/en/BWC/CONF.IX/2) applied mutatis mutandis, as confirmed in its organizational document BWC/WG/1/2 (available at https://docs.un.org/en/BWC/WG/1/2). Under these rules, the Working Group's final report—including any conclusions and recommendations—must be adopted by consensus. Through its first seven sessions (2023–2025), the Working Group developed a "rolling text" addressing various aspects of BWC strengthening. The Working Group was urged by the Ninth Review Conference to complete its tasks by the end of 2025 but did not achieve this. At its 7th session (December 2025), the Working Group adopted its procedural report by consensus but did not finalize its substantive report. At the 8th session (February 9–13, 2026, Geneva), the Working Group adopted a procedural report by consensus on February 13, 2026, and continued work on the rolling text, including discussion of verification-related provisions (e.g., "methods, procedures and techniques that could contribute to verification"), but did not adopt a final substantive report. The documents from this session are available at https://meetings.unoda.org/meeting/79376/documents, including the draft procedural report (BWC/WG/8/CRP.1/Rev.3) and a revised draft final report (BWC/WG/8/CRP.1). Reports from the session indicate growing support among delegations for recommending that the Tenth Review Conference establish an Open-Ended Working Group (OEWG) specifically dedicated to compliance and verification, though formal consensus on this point has not been reached and significant disagreements remain on the substance. Three remaining sessions are scheduled in 2026: - 9th session: August 17–21, 2026 - 10th session: December 7–11, 2026 - BWC Meeting of States Parties: December 14–16, 2026 Geopolitical tensions significantly complicate consensus-building. Russia—which has been assessed by some states as maintaining an active offensive bioweapons program—has historically resisted binding verification measures. China and other states have expressed concerns about intrusiveness. Western states and a majority of States Parties generally support stronger verification measures. However, the consensus requirement gives any single State Party present an effective veto over the final report.

Resolution criteria

This question resolves Yes if, between March 19, 2026 (00:00 UTC) and December 31, 2026 (23:59 UTC), the BWC Working Group on the Strengthening of the Convention adopts by consensus a final report that contains an explicit recommendation to establish a new subsidiary body—such as an Open-Ended Working Group (OEWG), Ad Hoc Committee, Ad Hoc Group, or equivalent institutional arrangement—with a specific mandate to develop, negotiate, or implement measures for verification of compliance with the BWC, or to develop a legally binding compliance or verification instrument. This question resolves No if any of the following conditions hold as of December 31, 2026 (23:59 UTC): - The Working Group does not adopt a consensus final report by that date; - The Working Group adopts a consensus final report that does not contain a recommendation to establish a new subsidiary body with a specific mandate focused on verification or compliance; or - The final report mentions verification or compliance only in general or aspirational terms (e.g., "States Parties should consider further discussions on verification") without recommending the establishment of a specific new dedicated body or institutional process.

Forecast rationale

This question asks whether the BWC Working Group will adopt a consensus final report with a specific recommendation to establish a new subsidiary body dedicated to verification/compliance by December 31, 2026. The resolution criteria are demanding: it requires not just any consensus report, but one with explicit language recommending a new body with a specific verification/compliance mandate. Factors favoring YES (~30% weight): - There is reported 'clear consensus' among many delegations that an OEWG on compliance and verification should be established [77190a, 2e556d] - Draft language already exists in the rolling text (BWC/WG/8/CRP.1/Rev.3) discussing establishment of such an OEWG [f2fbbb] - Historical precedent: VEREX (1991) and the Ad Hoc Group (1994) were both established by consensus, showing states can agree to create bodies even when disagreeing on substance - Strong institutional pressure to produce something tangible after 4 years (10 sessions) of work, especially before the Tenth Review Conference in 2027 - The 'process as progress' strategy - recommending a body is a lower bar than agreeing on verification substance itself - Russia and China have strategic reasons to support an OEWG (platform for their own concerns) Factors favoring NO (~70% weight): - After 8 sessions, no substantive final report has been adopted; the rolling text remains 'heavily bracketed' [654099, e61f82] - The consensus requirement gives any single state an effective veto - Russia, China, or even the US under the Trump administration could block - Irreconcilable scope disagreements persist: Articles I/II focus (Western) vs. Article X inclusion (NAM/China/Russia) [e61f82] - The 'legally-binding measures' language remains a poison pill [e61f82] - The 'thin gruel' problem: even if a report is adopted, consensus pressures may water down language to general/aspirational terms that wouldn't meet the specific resolution criteria - Expert opinion (Guthrie, Lentzos, Zanders) leans toward low probability for a substantive consensus report - Broader geopolitical tensions (Ukraine, US-China) reduce trust needed for agreement on verification - Only 2 substantive sessions remain (August, December 2026) - US policy under Trump administration is uncertain and may shift against multilateral verification bodies Key Uncertainties: 1. Russia's strategic calculus: If Russia sees more value in blocking than participating, it exercises its veto. If it sees OEWG as a platform for its own narratives, it may support. This single factor could swing probability ±15%. 2. Specificity vs. consensus tradeoff: The resolution criteria requires a 'specific mandate' for verification/compliance. Consensus may only be achievable with vaguer language. If the final text recommends 'further discussions' rather than a specific body, it resolves NO even if adopted. 3. US engagement level: Active US support for an OEWG would significantly boost chances; US withdrawal or opposition would likely doom it. 4. Last-session dynamics: Historical BWC practice shows some breakthroughs occur at final sessions under deadline pressure, but also shows that 'last-minute compromises' often gut substantive content. Base rate calibration: The 77% success rate for BWC Review Conference Final Declarations is relevant but overstates probability because those often include procedural elements, while this question demands specific verification language. The 0% success rate for legally binding verification protocol is somewhat relevant. Recent UN First Committee consensus rate of ~22% for disarmament resolutions provides a relevant environmental signal. I weight the VEREX/AHG precedent (100%) partially but discount it given the much more adversarial geopolitical environment. Expert/market signals: No direct prediction market exists. Expert opinion consistently leans toward low probability for substantive consensus. This is an important signal. Synthesizing all factors, I estimate approximately 15% probability. The broad conceptual support and existing draft language provide a non-trivial path to YES, but the consensus requirement, deep substantive disagreements, geopolitical barriers, and the demanding resolution criteria make NO the much more likely outcome.

Adversarial review PASS

Assessment: PASS

ASSESSMENT: PASS REVIEW: This is a well-crafted forecasting question addressing genuine uncertainty in multilateral arms control diplomacy. The key findings from my review: 1. Not already resolved: As of March 19, 2026, the Working Group has NOT adopted a final substantive report. At its 8th session (Feb 9-13, 2026), only a procedural report was adopted by consensus. Three sessions remain: 9th (Aug 17-21), 10th (Dec 7-11), and MSP (Dec 14-16). The question is genuinely open. 2. Schedule is accurate: The remaining session dates match official UNODA and UN Indico listings. 3. Background is largely accurate but slightly understates OEWG consensus: The CBW Events report from the 8th session states "There is a clear consensus that there should be an Open-Ended Working Group (OEWG) on compliance and verification established by the Tenth BWC Review Conference" [c70740]. The question's background characterizes this as merely "growing support," which may slightly understate the level of agreement on the OEWG concept itself. However, the same report confirms that significant disagreements remain on substance—specifically on the scope of verification (whether it covers only Articles I-II or broader provisions like Article X) and the legal nature of verification measures (legally-binding vs. non-legally-binding) [c70740]. This means the real uncertainty is less about whether an OEWG will be recommended and more about whether the entire final report can achieve consensus given these unresolved substantive disputes. 4. Resolution criteria are well-calibrated: The criteria properly distinguish between (a) adoption of a consensus final report (not just procedural reports), (b) containing an explicit recommendation for a new subsidiary body, (c) with a specific mandate on verification/compliance. The draft rolling text (BWC/WG/8/CRP.1/Rev.2) already contains language about an "Open-Ended Working Group on Compliance and Verification" with bracketed text indicating unresolved issues, so the question properly targets the remaining uncertainty. 5. Consensus rule correctly described: The consensus requirement under Rule 28 does apply to the substantive final report, not just procedural session reports. The question correctly identifies that any single State Party present can effectively block adoption. 6. Diplomatic terminology matches: The terms "subsidiary body," "Open-Ended Working Group," and "verification/compliance mandate" are consistent with the actual diplomatic language appearing in the BWC working papers and draft texts. 7. Forecasters should note: The question effectively functions as a compound forecast—(i) will the entire final report be adopted by consensus AND (ii) will it contain the specific OEWG recommendation. Based on available evidence, condition (ii) seems likely if condition (i) is met, since there's reportedly "clear consensus" on the OEWG concept [c70740]. The primary uncertainty is condition (i), driven by broader substantive disagreements. This is a feature of the question (it captures meaningful uncertainty), not a flaw, but forecasters should understand where the real risk lies. ▶ 11% Will the OSTP Framework's October 13, 2026 deadline for reducing DNA synthesis screening windows to 50 nucleotides be maintained without postponement or rescission by the Trump administration by December 31, 2026? Sourcelab_leaks Modelunknown/full REVISED Imp88.0 Qual92.0 Amb95.0

Proto-question Stage 1

Will the OSTP Framework's October 13, 2026 deadline for reducing DNA synthesis screening windows to 50 nucleotides be maintained without postponement or rescission by the Trump administration by December 31, 2026?

Why this question? The paper discusses DNA synthesis screening evasion as a key risk factor. The OSTP Framework set an October 13, 2026 deadline for providers to screen orders down to 50 nucleotides, a significant technical tightening. However, the Trump administration has shown a deregulatory stance on AI, and the Framework was issued under the Biden administration. Whether this deadline survives is a genuinely uncertain upstream indicator of biosecurity governance.

Paper reference: The paper discusses AI-designed sequences capable of 'evading commercial DNA synthesis screening' (Wittmann et al. 2025) and notes that 'improved real-time information sharing and screening of dual-use materials may increase the economic and political costs of bioweapon development' (Section 3).

Background

In September 2024, the White House Office of Science and Technology Policy (OSTP) released the revised Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening (the "2024 Framework"). This document established a phased timeline for providers of synthetic nucleic acids (DNA and RNA) and manufacturers of benchtop nucleic acid synthesis equipment. The Framework requires that entities receiving U.S. federal funding only procure synthetic nucleic acids from providers who adhere to specified screening standards. Central to these standards is the concept of a "screening window"—the minimum length of a nucleotide sequence that must be checked against databases of sequences of concern (SOCs), which include potential bioweapon agents. Under the 2024 Framework's phased timeline: - October 26, 2024: Initial implementation; providers must screen orders of 200 nucleotides or longer. - October 13, 2025: Screening window decreases; providers must screen each window of nucleotides within an order. - October 13, 2026: Screening window decreases to 50 nucleotides, requiring providers to screen each 50-nucleotide window for SOCs. This is the most technically demanding requirement and is the focus of this question. DNA synthesis screening is a key biosecurity measure designed to prevent malicious actors from ordering synthetic DNA sequences that encode dangerous pathogens or toxins. The 50-nucleotide threshold is significant because shorter sequences are harder to screen but can still be assembled into dangerous constructs, particularly with AI-assisted sequence design.

Resolution criteria

This question resolves YES if, as of December 31, 2026, 23:59 UTC, there exists an active, publicly available U.S. federal policy document that meets both of the following conditions: 1. It requires providers of synthetic nucleic acids (or any specific class thereof) who serve recipients of U.S. federal funding to screen nucleic acid orders at a window of 50 nucleotides or fewer for sequences of concern. The 50-nucleotide threshold (or equivalent length if different units are used, e.g., "base pairs") must be explicitly stated as a numeric value. If a policy does not specify a numeric screening window length, it does not satisfy this criterion. The requirement qualifies even if it only applies to a specific class of synthetic nucleic acid providers (e.g., double-stranded DNA providers), and need not apply to all provider types; AND 2. This requirement has an effective date on or before October 13, 2026, 23:59 UTC. Any effective date after October 13, 2026, 23:59 UTC results in a NO resolution, regardless of how close the date is to the deadline. Qualifying policy documents include any of the following: - A revised or replacement OSTP Framework or Memorandum issued under EO 14292; - The original 2024 OSTP Framework (September 2024 version), if it has not been formally superseded, rescinded, or revoked by a subsequent OSTP memorandum, executive order, or equivalent federal action, and it is affirmatively presented as active policy on at least one official federal implementation page (e.g., the ASPR/HHS page or equivalent). A framework that is merely archived or described solely as "under revision" without any affirmative statement that its requirements remain in effect does not qualify as active policy; - A Federal Register Notice establishing such a requirement; - An Executive Order establishing or codifying such a requirement; - A federal statute (Act of Congress) signed into law that establishes such a requirement. This question resolves NO if any of the following are true as of December 31, 2026, 23:59 UTC: - Rescinded: The 2024 Framework has been formally rescinded, revoked, or replaced by a document that does not include a 50-nucleotide screening requirement effective on or before October 13, 2026; OR - Postponed: The 2024 Framework or its replacement sets the effective date for the 50-nucleotide screening requirement to a date after October 13, 2026, 23:59 UTC; OR - Not affirmatively active: The 2024 Framework has not been formally rescinded but no official federal implementation page affirmatively presents the 50-nucleotide requirement as current, active policy (e.g., the requirement is described only as "under revision" or the implementation page states it will be updated once a new framework is available); OR - No active policy: No qualifying U.S. federal policy document containing a 50-nucleotide screening requirement with an effective date on or before October 13, 2026 can be identified.

Forecast rationale

This question asks whether, by December 31, 2026, an active U.S. federal policy will require 50-nucleotide screening with an effective date on or before October 13, 2026. The resolution criteria are quite specific and demanding. Current Status (Strong NO signal): The most critical factor is that the ASPR/HHS implementation page—the primary official federal implementation portal—currently describes the framework as in a 'state of transition' and states it will be updated once a new framework is available [fcc85b, af1946, a383f6]. Under the explicit resolution criteria, a framework described as 'under revision' without affirmative active status does NOT qualify as active policy. This alone currently satisfies the 'not affirmatively active' NO resolution condition. EO 14292 Impact: The Trump administration's Executive Order 14292 directed OSTP to 'revise or replace' the framework within 90 days (by ~August 3, 2025). That deadline passed over 7 months ago with no public replacement [b35193]. The administration's 'commonsense approach' language and aggressive deregulatory posture (129-to-1 ratio of eliminated to new regulations) suggest any replacement is unlikely to maintain the technically demanding 50-nt threshold with the original timeline. Legislative Path (S. 3741): The Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 was introduced January 29, 2026. However: (1) historical base rates for any bill becoming law are 1-4% per Congress; (2) the bill does NOT specify a 50-nucleotide threshold, instead delegating technical standards to Commerce with a 1-year rulemaking window [521ddc, e7da3f]; (3) even if passed, Commerce rulemaking would not produce a 50-nt requirement effective by October 13, 2026. The ClawdINT 60-65% passage estimate [cb3dab] seems optimistic, but even at face value, passage alone would not resolve YES. Agency Signals: NIH guidance may have superseded earlier framework references [d822b3]. DOE's FAL 2025-03 may still reference the framework [c77063], but a single agency is insufficient under the resolution criteria (need OSTP affirmation or majority of NIH/NSF/DOE/DOD). Key Uncertainties: 1. Will OSTP release a revised framework maintaining the 50-nt/Oct 2026 deadline? If yes, resolves YES. However, the missed deadline, deregulatory posture, and 'commonsense' language make this unlikely. If the revised framework softens or delays the requirement, it resolves NO. 2. Will the 'state of transition' persist through December 2026? If so, the 'not affirmatively active' criterion resolves NO. Given historical patterns of paused guidance remaining in limbo (60-80% probability), this is the most likely scenario. 3. Could rapid legislative action codify 50-nt screening? Even with bipartisan support, the bill's delegation of technical standards to future rulemaking and the 1-year implementation timeline make this path nearly impossible for meeting the October 2026 effective date. 4. Could a biosecurity emergency accelerate action? An actual bioterrorism event or credible AI-enabled threat could catalyze rapid policy action, but this is a low-probability tail event. Synthesis: The current state already effectively meets NO resolution criteria ('not affirmatively active'). For YES, something must actively change: either OSTP must issue a revised framework with the 50-nt requirement and original deadline, or agencies must affirmatively reinstate it. Given the deregulatory environment, missed deadlines, and the practical impossibility of the legislative path meeting the specific October 2026 effective date requirement, the probability is low.

Proto-question Stage 1

Will any frontier AI model achieve a score above 70% on SecureBio's Virology Capabilities Test (VCT) by December 31, 2026?

Why this question? The paper highlights AI-enabled virology uplift as a key risk pathway (Table 9 mentions 'LLMs provide substantial high-risk virology uplift, e.g. >80% accuracy on VCT'). The VCT is a real benchmark published by SecureBio that measures AI capability to troubleshoot complex virology lab protocols. As of early 2026, the highest VCT score is 0.610 (61%) by Grok-4.1 Thinking. Given rapid AI capability improvements, whether models will cross the 70% threshold is genuinely uncertain and would represent a significant capability milestone in the paper's risk framework.

Paper reference: Table 9 identifies 'LLMs provide substantial high-risk virology uplift (e.g. >80% accuracy on VCT, wet-lab studies)' as a potential red line scenario that could affect P(BW) and P(PPP). Section 3 discusses AI as a 'Lab Coach' (Table 4, scenario 2C) providing cross-domain assistance with wet lab protocols.

Background

The Virology Capabilities Test (VCT) is an expert-level, multimodal multiple-choice benchmark developed by SecureBio and the Center for AI Safety (CAIS) that measures an AI model's capability to troubleshoot complex virology laboratory protocols. The benchmark was published in 2025 and is available at https://www.virologytest.ai/. VCT is designed to assess dual-use virology knowledge — the kind of practical, contextualized assistance that could lower barriers to dangerous biological research. Expert virologists with internet access score an average of 22.1% on questions specifically within their sub-areas of expertise, making this a challenging benchmark even for human domain experts. The full multimodal VCT consists of 322 expert-validated virology questions, including both text-based and image-based questions. A separate text-only subset (VCT-Text, 101 questions) exists, on which AI models typically score substantially higher than on the full multimodal benchmark. This question resolves exclusively on the full multimodal VCT (322 questions), not VCT-Text, in order to maintain a well-calibrated threshold. VCT is referenced in the biosecurity literature as a key indicator of AI-enabled biological uplift risk. For instance, one prominent risk framework identifies "LLMs provide substantial high-risk virology uplift, e.g. >80% accuracy on VCT" as a potential red-line scenario that could materially affect the probability of catastrophic biological events.

Resolution criteria

This question resolves Yes if, between March 19, 2026 and December 31, 2026, 11:59 PM UTC, any frontier AI model (as defined above) achieves an accuracy score strictly greater than 70% (i.e., >0.700) on the full multimodal SecureBio Virology Capabilities Test (VCT, 322 questions), as reported in any of the following credible sources: 1. The official VCT website (https://www.virologytest.ai/) or any official SecureBio publication; 2. The llm-stats.com VCT benchmark leaderboard (https://llm-stats.com/benchmarks/vct); 3. An AI developer's official system card or model card (e.g., published by OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, xAI, etc.); 4. A peer-reviewed or preprint academic publication (e.g., on arXiv) that reports VCT evaluation results.

Forecast rationale

Synthesis and Rationale: This question asks whether any frontier AI model will score above 70% on the full multimodal VCT (322 questions) before the end of 2026. As of March 19, 2026, the best publicly reported score is 61.0% by Grok-4.1 Thinking, requiring a 9 percentage point improvement within ~9.3 months. Historical trajectory is the strongest YES signal. Performance jumped from 43.8% (o3, early 2025) to 61.0% (Grok-4.1 Thinking, late 2025) — a 17.2pp gain in ~7 months. Even at half this rate (~1.2pp/month), the 70% mark would be reached within the window. The introduction of "thinking" / test-time-compute architectures was the primary driver, and this paradigm continues to scale. Max effort configurations are already approaching mid-60s. The research mentions Claude Opus 4.6 achieving ~65.4% with max effort configurations, which is only 4.6pp below the threshold. If newer models (GPT-5.4, Grok-5, Gemini 3.x/4) show similar or larger gains in their max-effort variants, crossing 70% becomes quite plausible. Multiple shots on goal. With 9+ months remaining and expected releases from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, xAI, and others, there will be at least 3-5 major frontier model releases, each representing an independent opportunity to cross the threshold. GPT-5.4 has already launched but lacks official VCT evaluation scores. However, the multimodal gap is real. The full VCT includes ~221 image-based questions requiring interpretation of lab imagery (gel electrophoresis, microscopy, schematics). Text-only VCT-Text scores are substantially higher, suggesting the visual reasoning component is a genuine bottleneck. Newer models (Claude Opus 4.6 at 48.3%, Gemini 3 Pro at 56.2%) have not beaten Grok-4.1's 61% on the full multimodal set, suggesting potential plateauing. Safety guardrails could depress scores. As VCT explicitly measures dual-use virology knowledge, labs may implement refusal behaviors for the most dangerous (and complex) questions, artificially lowering scores. Market signal: Metaculus community prediction is at ~35-40% [8db03a], reflecting cautious outlook. I diverge upward because: (1) the historical improvement rate strongly supports crossing 70%, (2) max-effort scores already near 65%, and (3) multiple unreleased/unevaluated next-gen models provide many opportunities. Key Uncertainties: 1. Is the ~61% score a genuine plateau or evaluation lag? If newer models (GPT-5.4, upcoming releases) simply haven't been evaluated yet, the effective SOTA may already be higher. If the plateau is real, crossing 70% becomes harder. 2. Will multimodal visual reasoning improve substantially? This is the core bottleneck. If native multimodal training improves significantly in 2026, the gap closes quickly. If not, the visual questions remain a ceiling. 3. Will safety considerations prevent high scores from being reported? Labs facing pressure around biological uplift risk may choose not to report scores, or may train models to refuse dual-use questions. 4. Will evaluation methodology remain strict? The resolution criteria exclude agentic/multi-tool approaches. If the only scores above 70% come from non-qualifying evaluation setups, the question resolves NO. Balancing the strong historical trajectory and multiple upcoming model releases against the multimodal ceiling, market skepticism, and safety considerations, I estimate this at moderately above the market consensus.

Adversarial review NEEDS_REVISION

Assessment: NEEDS_REVISION

ASSESSMENT: NEEDS_REVISION REVIEW: The question addresses a genuinely important and uncertain topic, and the VCT benchmark, arXiv paper (2504.16137), and llm-stats.com leaderboard all exist as described. However, there are several substantive issues: 1. VCT-Text eligibility undermines the threshold calibration. The question allows resolution on either the full multimodal VCT (322 questions) or VCT-Text (101 text-only questions). The 70% threshold appears calibrated to the full VCT, where the best score is ~61%. However, VCT-Text scores are typically significantly higher than full VCT scores (the EA Forum notes "4-fold" improvement on VCT-Text over the study period). The Claude Opus 4.6 System Card reports a 48.3% score on the full multimodal VCT [a252f8], yet the question claims 65.4% "with max effort" — this higher number likely comes from VCT-Text or a different evaluation variant, not the full multimodal benchmark. Allowing VCT-Text means the effective gap to 70% may be much smaller than the background section implies, potentially making this near-trivially YES. 2. The 65.4% claim from the Opus 4.6 System Card may be inaccurate or misleading. When I queried the actual Claude Opus 4.6 System Card PDF, the VCT score reported was 0.483 (48.3%) on the "multiple select" variant [a252f8]. A second query specifically looking for the 65.4% figure found no such VCT score in the document [8cbd75]. The 65.4% may refer to a different benchmark or a different evaluation setup than what the question implies. This factual error in the background section would mislead forecasters about the current state of play. 3. Conflation of VCT and VCT-Text in the "Current Status" section. The background presents "~61–65%" as the range of current top scores, but this conflates full VCT scores (Grok-4.1 Thinking at 61.0%) with what may be VCT-Text or differently-configured evaluation scores (the 65.4% claim). This creates a misleading impression of the gap to the 70% threshold. 4. The llm-stats.com leaderboard is extremely sparse. Google snippets indicate it currently "ranks 1 AI models" on VCT, making it a very limited resolution source. The question relies heavily on this leaderboard but it may not track new models promptly. 5. The "quadrupled from early 2025" framing is misleading. The background states "VCT top scores roughly quadrupled from early 2025 (o3 at 43.8%) to late 2025 (Grok-4.1 Thinking at 61.0%)." Going from 43.8% to 61.0% is roughly a 39% increase, not quadrupling. The "quadrupled" claim likely refers to VCT-Text scores specifically, further conflating the two benchmarks. EVIDENCE: https://llm-stats.com/benchmarks/vct https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16137 https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/14e4fb01875d2a69f646fa5e574dea2b1c0ff7b5.pdf https://www.virologytest.ai/ https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/5EhJhMAdjncpSzzEL/llms-outperform-experts-on-challenging-biology-benchmarks https://data.x.ai/2025-11-17-grok-4-1-model-card.pdf SUGGESTION: 1. Either restrict resolution to only the full multimodal VCT (322 questions), or set separate thresholds for VCT and VCT-Text. The two benchmarks have substantially different difficulty levels and conflating them makes the threshold poorly calibrated. If VCT-Text is included, the threshold should be higher (e.g., 80%) to maintain genuine uncertainty. 2. Correct or remove the 65.4% claim. The Claude Opus 4.6 System Card appears to report 48.3% on the full multimodal VCT, not 65.4%. Either verify the source of this number and correctly attribute it to the specific benchmark version, or remove it. 3. Fix the "quadrupled" claim. Full VCT scores went from ~43.8% to ~61.0%, which is not quadrupling. If referencing VCT-Text progress, label it clearly. 4. Revise the current status section to clearly separate full VCT scores from VCT-Text scores, so forecasters can accurately assess the gap to the threshold on each version.

Proto-question Stage 1

Will the "Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026" (S.3741) or a successor bill containing a mandate for DNA synthesis screening be signed into US law by 31st December 2027?

Why this question? The podcast discusses the need for "damage control" and "defusing the bomb" of biological risk [Page 67]. Research reveals that bipartisan legislation (S.3741) was introduced in early 2026 specifically to mandate that gene synthesis providers screen orders for dangerous sequences—a key policy milestone for mitigating AI-enabled biorisks. Its passage would represent a major regulatory response to the threats described.

Paper reference: Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 (S.3741) provisions regarding DNA synthesis screening.

Background

As of April 1, 2026, the regulation of synthetic DNA in the United States is transitioning from voluntary frameworks to mandatory requirements. While the 2023 HHS Screening Framework Guidance established best practices, the May 5, 2025, Executive Order, "Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research," mandated that federal agencies ensure synthetic nucleic acid procurement is conducted through providers adhering to an updated screening framework Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research. Furthermore, the Executive Order directed the development of a strategy to govern non-federally funded research and mandated the submission of a legislative proposal to address gaps in authority to achieve comprehensive, scalable, and verifiable nucleic acid synthesis screening in non-federally funded settings Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research. The "Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026" (S.3741), introduced on January 29, 2026, by Senator Tom Cotton (R-AR) and co-sponsored by Senator Amy Klobuchar (D-MN), serves as the legislative vehicle for this administration-backed initiative to extend mandatory screening requirements to the entire industry S.3741 - Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 ... Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research. This forecasting question tracks whether the U.S. will successfully enact this mandatory, legally enforceable system for DNA synthesis screening before the end of 2027.

Resolution criteria

This question will resolve as Yes if, between January 1, 2026, and 23:59 UTC on December 31, 2027, the Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 (S.3741) or a successor bill is "signed into law" by the President of the United States or otherwise enacted via constitutional processes. * DNA Synthesis Screening: Defined as the process of identifying whether a requested or synthesized nucleic acid sequence matches a "Sequence of Concern" (SOC) to prevent the misuse of synthetic biology for creating pathogens or toxins. * Mandatory Requirement: The enacted legislation must contain a provision that makes screening and/or customer verification a legal requirement for "covered providers" (entities synthesizing/selling synthetic nucleic acids or benchtop synthesis equipment). The mandate must apply to all "covered providers" as defined in S.3741, regardless of revenue or organizational size, including both synthesis services and benchtop equipment manufacturers S.3741 - Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 .... The legislation must impose a direct legal obligation on the private-sector providers themselves, rather than solely restricting federal procurement or agency behavior. * Penalties: The penalties for non-compliance must include punitive measures such as civil fines, statutory damages (e.g., as described in Section 4(f) of S.3741), or revocation of operating licenses, rather than just a loss of optional federal funding S.3741 - Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 .... * Successor Bill: A bill qualifies as a successor if it originates from the same legislative intent as S.3741, regardless of its final bill number or title, provided it retains the core mandate for DNA synthesis screening. The bill must retain a requirement to screen against a comprehensive and evolving list of sequences of concern that includes, but is not limited to, the Pathogens and Toxins of Biosecurity Concern. * Signed into Law: This includes the President signing the bill, the bill becoming law without a signature after 10 days while Congress is in session, or Congress successfully overriding a presidential veto. The legislation qualifies for a YES resolution if it is enacted by the deadline, even if the implementation date or the date on which penalties become enforceable occurs after December 31, 2027. * Resolution Source: The official status and text of the legislation as tracked on Congress.gov for bill S.3741 (119th Congress) or its successors. The "All Actions" and "Text" tabs will be used to verify enactment and the presence of the mandatory screening provision.

Adversarial review NEEDS_REVISION

Assessment: NEEDS_REVISION

ASSESSMENT: NEEDS_REVISION REVIEW: The forecasting question is technically well-defined but contains a significant gap in its background section that affects the framing of the uncertainty. 1. Existence of Bill and Sponsors: The Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 (S.3741) and its sponsors (Senators Cotton and Klobuchar) are real S.3741 - Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 .... The bill was introduced on January 29, 2026, and includes mandatory screening requirements with civil penalties (up to $750,000 for non-individuals) S.3741 - Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 .... 2. Current Regulatory Landscape: The background correctly identifies the 2023 HHS Screening Framework as a voluntary guideline https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Pages/Synthetic-Nucleic-Acids.aspx. However, it fails to mention the May 5, 2025, Executive Order, "Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research" Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research. 3. Substantive Problem: This Executive Order already mandates DNA synthesis screening for all federally funded research and, crucially, directed the OSTP to submit a legislative proposal by November 2025 to cover non-federally funded settings Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research. S.3741 appears to be the bipartisan legislative vehicle for this administration-backed initiative. 4. Impact on Forecasters: By presenting the bill as a new attempt to fix a "gap" in a "voluntary regime," the background ignores that the transition to a mandatory regime is already official executive policy. This makes the bill more likely to pass (as it has administration support and bipartisan sponsorship) than the current text suggests. The "uncertainty" is less about whether the U.S. wants a mandatory system and more about the legislative timing of a pre-planned policy shift. 5. Resolution Criteria: The criteria for "successor bill" and "mandatory requirement" are objective and provide clear guardrails for resolution S.3741 - Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 .... Proto-question Stage 1

Will a publicly available AI model achieve a score of 60% or higher on the Virology Capabilities Test (VCT) (or a successor version using the same methodology) by December 31, 2027?

Why this question? Current state-of-the-art models like OpenAI's o3 have achieved 43.8% on the VCT, which already outperforms 94% of expert virologists [cfc167]. Reaching 60% would represent a significant leap in the model's ability to provide 'tacit' laboratory knowledge that was previously thought to be a human-only barrier. This is a non-trivial milestone that tracks the rapid advancement of dual-use biological capabilities in LLMs.

Paper reference: Virology Capabilities Test (VCT) and Frontier AI model performance [cfc167]

Background

The Virology Capabilities Test (VCT) is a specialized benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to troubleshoot complex virology laboratory protocols Virology Capabilities Test. Developed by PhD-level virologists, the test consists of 322 multimodal questions (including text and images) that probe "tacit" laboratory knowledge—practical, expert-level information often absent from standard textbooks but critical for successful biological experimentation Virology Capabilities Test Virology Capabilities Test. As of April 1, 2026, the state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on the VCT is 43.8%, achieved by OpenAI's o3 model Virology Capabilities Test Virology Capabilities Test. This score already exceeds the performance of approximately 94% of human expert virologists tested on the same material Virology Capabilities Test. Crossing the 60% threshold would represent a significant leap in AI capability, potentially signaling that AI systems can provide expert-level guidance on dual-use biological procedures that were previously considered protected by a human "knowledge barrier" Virology Capabilities Test.

Resolution criteria

This question will resolve as YES if, at any point between April 1, 2026, and December 31, 2027, at 23:59 UTC, a publicly available AI model is verified to have achieved a score of 60.0% or higher on the Virology Capabilities Test (VCT) or a qualified successor version. 1. Publicly Available Model: A model is considered "publicly available" if it is accessible to the general public via a web interface (e.g., ChatGPT, Claude.ai, Gemini), a publicly documented API (e.g., OpenAI API, Anthropic API), or if its weights are available for public download (e.g., Llama, Mistral). Models accessible only to internal researchers or government-vetted partners do not count. 2. Successor Version Criteria: If the original VCT (v1.0) is updated or replaced by its original authors (at SecureBio or the Center for AI Safety) before the resolution date, the question will resolve based on the performance on the most recent version (e.g., VCT v2.0), provided it maintains the same core methodology of measuring expert-level troubleshooting of virology protocols through multimodal Q&A Virology Capabilities Test. 3. Verification of Achievement: Achievement of the score must be verified by one of the following: * Publication on the official VCT leaderboard at virologytest.ai Virology Capabilities Test. * A technical report or peer-reviewed paper from the model's developer (e.g., OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind) citing the VCT score. * An independent evaluation published by a recognized AI safety or evaluation organization (e.g., METR, UK AI Safety Institute, US AI Safety Institute). 4. Resolution Source: The primary source for resolution is the official leaderboard at https://www.virologytest.ai/. In the event the leaderboard is defunct, resolution will rely on the original research paper "Virology Capabilities Test (VCT): A Multimodal Virology Q&A Benchmark" (arXiv:2504.16137) or credible reporting from major outlets (e.g., Reuters, Nature, The New York Times) Virology Capabilities Test Virology Capabilities Test.

Forecast rationale

Time left: ~21 months (until Dec 31, 2027). Status quo: State-of-the-art performance is currently 43.8% (OpenAI o3), though research notes xAI's Grok-4.1 Thinking has reportedly already achieved a score of 61.0%. Scope: Any public model verified via leaderboard, developer technical report, or independent evaluator. A No outcome would require Grok-4.1's reported score to be unverified or retracted, combined with a severe, unexpected plateau in multimodal reasoning capabilities for all upcoming models (e.g., GPT-5, Claude 4) over the next two years. A Yes outcome is extremely likely given historical base rates; AI benchmarks like GPQA have seen leaps of nearly 50 points in a single year, and the VCT improved by 25 points in just 18 months. With a mere 16.2% gap from o3's score to the 60% threshold, and reports indicating that next-gen models have already crossed it, the trajectory is clear. In terms of bets, I would happily pay 94 cents for a contract that pays out $1 on a Yes, as the threshold is highly likely to be officially verified well before the deadline.
