Proto-question Stage 1

By December 31, 2026, will the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) or the Office of Science and Technology Policy (OSTP) publish a final, updated "Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening" that requires federally funded institutions to only purchase from providers that screen all orders?

Why this question? The paper identifies "ordering from DNA synthesis companies who don't screen" as a primary fear. A updated framework is currently being revised following a May 2025 Executive Order [9b9597]. This question tracks a critical regulatory milestone that would address the "institutional and coordinative" bottlenecks mentioned in the paper.

Paper reference: Conclusion, Page 66-67: "ordering from DNA synthesis companies who don't screen" and the need for "comprehensive coverage."

Background

In May 2025, Executive Order 14292, "Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research," mandated the revision or replacement of the September 2024 "Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening" [[PDF] Framework For Nucleic Acid Synthesi Screening](https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf). While the 2024 Framework established a baseline for procurement, the 2025 Executive Order explicitly requires the updated framework to incorporate "verifiable" screening mechanisms and specific enforcement terms (as detailed in Section 7 of the Order) into Federal funding agreements Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research. As of March 31, 2026, the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) and the Office of Science and Technology Policy (OSTP) are in the process of finalizing this update. To be a meaningful forecast, this question targets the new requirements sought by the 2025 Executive Order that go beyond the 2024 baseline. Specifically, it looks for the inclusion of verifiable screening mechanisms, specific enforcement mechanisms, or expanded technical standards.

Resolution criteria

This question resolves as YES if, between April 1, 2026, and 23:59 UTC on December 31, 2026, the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) or the Office of Science and Technology Policy (OSTP) publishes a final, updated "Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening" (or a successor document) that meets the following conditions: 1. Enhanced Requirements: The framework must include at least one of the following novel elements mandated by Executive Order 14292: * Verifiable Screening: The framework explicitly mandates "verifiable" screening mechanisms (e.g., third-party audits or standardized reporting of screening efficacy) Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research. * Enforcement Mechanisms: The framework explicitly incorporates the enforcement mechanisms described in Section 7 of Executive Order 14292, such as requiring grant recipients to certify compliance and establishing that violations may lead to the revocation of funding or up to a 5-year period of ineligibility for future grants Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research. 2. Comprehensive Screening: The framework must mandate screening for all three types of nucleic acids: double-stranded DNA, single-stranded DNA, and RNA [[PDF] Framework For Nucleic Acid Synthesi Screening](https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf). 3. Applicability: The requirement must apply to "Non-Federal entities" (including Recipients or Subrecipients of federal research funding) as defined in 2 CFR § 200.1. 4. Final Version: The document must be identified as "Final" or a "Revision" that replaces the September 2024 version. An "Interim Final" or "Final" document that establishes a compliance date shall count as "Final" even if it remains open for public comment. Clarifications: * Publication Rule: The first appearance of the document on the official HHS/ASPR website, the White House/OSTP website, or the Federal Register within the window constitutes publication. * Waivers: The "only" or "exclusively" purchase requirement is satisfied if the framework establishes compliant-provider use as the mandatory default policy, even if it allows for narrow, documented emergency or national security waivers. * Incorporation by Reference: The "comprehensive screening" requirement is met if the framework incorporates external technical standards (such as NIST or IGSC) by reference that contain the necessary protocols. If no such final framework is published, or if the published framework lacks the "verifiable" requirement, the Section 7 enforcement mechanisms, or fails to cover all three nucleic acid types, this question resolves as NO.

Forecast rationale

(a) Time left: There are 275 days left until the end of 2026. (b) Status quo: The 2024 Framework remains the active baseline, with the mandated update still in the drafting phase Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research. (c) Scope: The executive branch has significant leeway to publish an 'Interim Final' framework to meet deadlines, even if technical details are still being refined. (d) No reasoning: Bureaucratic delays, incomplete NIST technical standards [[PDF] Framework For Nucleic Acid Synthesi Screening](https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf), and pushback from institutions regarding the severe Section 7 enforcement penalties Why implementation gaps could undermine synthetic nucleic acid ... could easily push the publication into 2027. Federal agencies routinely miss deadlines https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Pages/Synthetic-Nucleic-Acid-Screening.aspx. (e) Yes reasoning: Executive Order 14292 explicitly mandates these updates Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research. Since the 2024 Framework already established a baseline for dsDNA, ssDNA, and RNA [[PDF] Framework For Nucleic Acid Synthesi Screening](https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf), adding verifiable screening and enforcement provisions is a targeted update rather than a ground-up rewrite. (f) Betting: I would be indifferent to taking either side of a bet at a 63% probability. Final probability: 63%

Adversarial review NEEDS_REVISION

Assessment: NEEDS_REVISION

ASSESSMENT: NEEDS_REVISION REVIEW: The forecasting question requires revision because its primary condition—that federally funded entities must purchase only from compliant providers—is already a feature of the existing September 2024 Framework [[PDF] Framework For Nucleic Acid Synthesi Screening](https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf). Section II of the 2024 Framework explicitly states that federal funding agencies will require 'synthetic nucleic acid procurement for federally funded research is conducted through Providers or Manufacturers that adhere to the framework,' a requirement that took effect on April 26, 2025 [[PDF] Framework For Nucleic Acid Synthesi Screening](https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf). While Executive Order 14292 (issued May 5, 2025) does mandate a revision or replacement of this framework Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research, the specific 'mandatory' purchasing requirement described in the resolution criteria is already active policy under the 2024 version. Consequently, a 'YES' resolution could be triggered by a document that merely restates existing requirements rather than introducing the intended 'more rigorous approach' mentioned in the background. Additionally, the technical definition of 'comprehensive screening' in the question matches the 'Best Match' homology search already recommended in the 2023 and 2024 guidance [[PDF] Screening Framework Guidance for Providers and Users of ...](https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/SynNA-Guidance-2023.pdf) [[PDF] Framework For Nucleic Acid Synthesi Screening](https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf). To be a meaningful forecast, the question needs to distinguish the new requirements sought by the 2025 Executive Order (such as 'verifiable' screening or specific enforcement mechanisms) from the baseline established in 2024. EVIDENCE: https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf, https://www.whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/2025/05/improving-the-safety-and-security-of-biological-research/, https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/SynNA-Guidance-2023.pdf SUGGESTION: Revise the resolution criteria to focus on the novel elements mandated by Executive Order 14292 that are not in the 2024 Framework. Specifically, require that the updated framework include 'verifiable' screening mechanisms or the specific 'enforcement mechanisms' described in Section 7 of EO 14292. Alternatively, pivot the question to focus on the inclusion of specific new technical standards (e.g., screening against 'functional' attributes rather than just homology) or the expansion of the 'Sequences of Concern' list to include specific AI-generated or synthetic threats mentioned in the 2025 Order.

SQ1: What is the current status of the technical standards and "verifiable" screening protocols mandated for the updated U.S. Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening Framework?
As of March 31, 2026, the updated U.S. Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening mandated by Executive Order 14292 (May 5, 2025) has not been finalized, following the expiration of the 90-day revision deadline in August 2025 Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research Regulatory Gaps in Benchtop Nucleic Acid Synthesis Create .... Consequently, there are currently no binding, government-mandated "verifiable" screening mechanisms, such as third-party audits, in effect for the general industry Regulatory Gaps in Benchtop Nucleic Acid Synthesis Create .... However, the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) has made significant progress on the technical foundation for these standards, including the development of a "fit-for-purpose" benchmark dataset to test screening tools and a draft standard guide to harmonize provider protocols Biosecurity for Synthetic Nucleic Acid Sequences | NIST. Key technical standards scheduled for implementation by October 13, 2026, include narrowing the screening window to 50 nucleotides, expanding the definition of "Sequences of Concern" to focus on functional pathogenicity, and implementing protocols to detect the assembly of shorter sequences into harmful agents [[PDF] Strengthening a Safe and Secure Nucleic Acid Synthesis Ecosystem](https://ebrc.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/02/EBRC-2025-Strengthening-a-Safe-and-Secure-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Ecosystem.pdf). While the NIH and other federal agencies have moved to adopt the 2024 Framework's baseline, the broader transition to a "verifiable" enforcement model remains in a period of regulatory development.
SQ2: How are the "Section 7" enforcement mechanisms and grant-compliance certifications being integrated into the revised Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening Framework and associated federal funding regulations?
Executive Order 14292, issued on May 5, 2025, mandates that 'Section 7' enforcement mechanisms be integrated into all federal life-science research funding. These mechanisms transform biosecurity compliance into a 'material condition for federal payment' by invoking the False Claims Act, making non-compliance a basis for legal prosecution Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research (Trump EO .... Grant recipients must now provide formal certifications that they do not participate in or fund 'dangerous gain-of-function' research or high-risk foreign research Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research. Enforcement is bolstered by severe penalties, including the immediate revocation of current funding and a potential 5-year period of ineligibility for future federal life-sciences grants, a penalty that can apply to entire institutions for the actions of individual recipients [[PDF] May 2025 Update Final - COGR](https://www.cogr.edu/sites/default/files/May%202025%20Update%20Final.pdf) Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research (Trump EO .... Regulatory bodies such as the NIH must update the NIH Grants Policy Statement to reflect these terms, following a timeline that required the OSTP to replace existing screening frameworks by August 2025 [[PDF] May 2025 Update Final - COGR](https://www.cogr.edu/sites/default/files/May%202025%20Update%20Final.pdf). Stakeholders like the Engineering Biology Research Consortium (EBRC) and the Council on Governmental Relations (COGR) have focused their feedback on the need for 'reasonable' screening strategies and have noted the significant administrative hurdles posed by institutional liability and the threat of long-term debarment Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening elements of EO 14292: Improving ... [[PDF] May 2025 Update Final - COGR](https://www.cogr.edu/sites/default/files/May%202025%20Update%20Final.pdf).

The divergence between the structural estimate (58%) and holistic estimate (62%) is small (4 points), so the final forecast is a simple average. The higher holistic view is weighted by the fact that even an 'Interim Final' rule satisfies the criteria, which is a common and efficient path for the US government to meet such deadlines.

Proto-question Stage 1

Will the 'Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026' (S. 3741), or a successor bill containing its core gene synthesis screening mandates, be signed into law by the President of the United States on or before December 31, 2026?

Why this question? The paper identifies a critical regulatory gap where current U.S. DNA synthesis screening is largely voluntary [ad2493]. The Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act, introduced in February 2026 by Senators Cotton and Klobuchar, represents the primary legislative attempt to close this gap by mandating screening for all synthetic DNA orders [d55ce2]. This question tracks the transition from a voluntary to a mandatory 'Swiss-cheese' layer of defense.

Paper reference: Section 3: Conclusion (part 19-21), Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 (S. 3741)

Forecast rationale

(a) Time left: 275 days until December 31, 2026. (b) Status quo: S. 3741 is in committee and has no House companion bill. If nothing changes, it dies in committee. (c) Scope: This involves passing a bicameral federal law, which requires overcoming numerous political and procedural hurdles during a midterm election year. (d) Why NO: Base rates for introduced bills passing are extremely low (1-4%). S. 3741 lacks a House companion and is a complex regulatory bill that could face industry pushback. Passing such a bill within 11 months of introduction is historically rare; the similar BIOSECURE Act took 23 months. (e) Why YES: Bipartisan support and national security framing could see its core mandates attached to a 'must-pass' vehicle like the FY2027 NDAA late in the year. (f) Bets: I would be indifferent between a 12-cent bet on YES and an 88-cent bet on NO.

This makes the introduction of S. 3741 (on January 29, 2026) a likely byproduct of an existing administrative mandate rather than an independent legislative effort S.3741 - Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 .... This significantly lowers the uncertainty for forecasters who are aware of the EO. 3. Successor Bill Ambiguity: The 'successor bill' definition is overly broad. Proto-question Stage 1

Will a peer-reviewed publication demonstrate an AI model generating a functional genome of a mammalian-infecting virus (not limited to bacteriophages) by December 31, 2026?

Why this question? The paper describes a scenario where AI 'identifies a mammalian poxvirus candidate and guides a bioengineering strategy to generate a novel capsid variant.' In 2025, Stanford researchers demonstrated AI-generated functional bacteriophage genomes using Evo models, but these are far simpler than mammalian viruses. Whether AI crosses the threshold to design functional mammalian virus genomes is a critical capability indicator for the paper's threat model.

Paper reference: The qualitative scenario in Section 4 describes AI identifying 'a mammalian poxvirus candidate' and guiding design of a novel variant. Table 4 lists 'Threat Enhancement' (scenario 2A) where AI is used to increase agent capabilities. The paper also references King et al. 2025 showing generative AI tools can generate 'novel, functional viral genomes.'

Background

In September 2025, researchers at Stanford University and the Arc Institute posted a landmark preprint (King et al., "Generative design of novel bacteriophages with genome language models," bioRxiv, September 17, 2025) reporting the first-ever AI-generated functional viral genomes. Using fine-tuned versions of the Evo 1 and Evo 2 genome language models—transformer-based foundation models trained on millions of prokaryotic and phage genomes—the team generated approximately 302 candidate bacteriophage genomes modeled on ΦX174, a virus that infects E. coli. Of these, 16 proved viable after synthesis and experimental testing, producing functional phages capable of killing bacteria. Several designs exhibited substantial evolutionary novelty, with genome sequences sharing less than 95% identity with any known natural phage genome. The Evo 2 model itself was published in Nature in March 2026 (Brixi et al., "Genome modelling and design across all domains of life with Evo 2," Nature, 2026), demonstrating capabilities to generate mitochondrial, prokaryotic, and eukaryotic sequences at genome scale with a context window of up to one million nucleotides. A separate January 2026 preprint ("Fundamental limitations of genomic language models," bioRxiv, 2026) identified key barriers to scaling these approaches to more complex organisms. These results represent a critical inflection point for AI-enabled biosecurity risks. Papers such as Brent & McKelvey (2025, "Contemporary AI foundation models increase biological weapons risk," arXiv:2506.13798) describe scenarios in which AI models guide the design of mammalian-infecting pathogens, including novel poxvirus variants. The King et al. bacteriophage result demonstrates proof-of-concept for AI genome generation, but bacteriophages (~5 kb genomes infecting bacteria) are far simpler than most mammalian-infecting viruses in terms of host interaction complexity, immune evasion mechanisms, and regulatory architecture—even though some mammalian viruses (e.g., parvoviruses) have comparably small genomes (~5 kb). As of March 2026, the King et al. bacteriophage study appears to remain a preprint and has not yet been published in a peer-reviewed journal. No published study has demonstrated AI generation of a functional mammalian-infecting virus genome. A March 2026 Nature news feature ("AI can write genomes—how long until it creates synthetic life?") noted that significant further advances are needed to move from short phage genomes to more complex living systems.

Resolution criteria

This question resolves Yes if, by December 31, 2026, 11:59 PM UTC, a peer-reviewed publication meets ALL of the following criteria: 1. AI Generative Model: The study uses an AI generative model to produce the viral genome sequence. An "AI generative model" is defined as a machine learning system that uses neural network architectures (e.g., transformers, variational autoencoders, diffusion models, or generative adversarial networks) to generate novel nucleotide sequences de novo or with minimal human-specified constraints. This includes models such as DNA/protein language models (e.g., Evo, Evo 2, megaDNA, or successors), but excludes: (a) traditional bioinformatics tools that perform sequence assembly, alignment, or homology-based reconstruction without learned generative components; (b) simple computational mutagenesis (e.g., scripted point mutations or recombination of known sequences without a trained generative model); and (c) purely predictive models that score or rank sequences but do not generate them. 2. Mammalian-Infecting Virus: The generated genome encodes a virus belonging to a species or genus that is classified by the International Committee on Taxonomy of Viruses (ICTV) as infecting mammalian hosts (class Mammalia, per the standard biological classification; see https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal). The ICTV's Virus Metadata Resource (VMR, available at https://ictv.global/vmr) serves as the authoritative reference for virus-host associations. If the generated virus represents a novel species not yet classified by the ICTV, its closest taxonomic relative (determined by standard phylogenetic methods as reported in the publication) must belong to a genus or family with established mammalian hosts per the VMR. Bacteriophages (viruses infecting bacteria) and viruses infecting only non-mammalian hosts (plants, insects, birds exclusively, etc.) do not qualify. 3. Functional Genome: The AI-generated genome must be demonstrated as "functional" through empirical wet-lab validation in mammalian cell cultures. Specifically, the publication must report at least ONE of the following experimentally verified outcomes: - (a) Successful replication: The synthesized genome directs autonomous replication of viral nucleic acid in mammalian cells, as confirmed by quantitative PCR, sequencing, or equivalent molecular assays; - (b) Viral protein expression: The genome directs expression of viral structural or non-structural proteins in mammalian cells, as confirmed by Western blot, mass spectrometry, immunofluorescence, or equivalent protein detection methods; - (c) Production of viral particles: The genome directs assembly of virus-like particles or infectious virions in mammalian cells, as confirmed by electron microscopy, plaque assays, TCID50, or equivalent virological methods. 4. Sequence Novelty: The AI-generated genome must exhibit less than 90% nucleotide sequence identity to any single known natural virus genome deposited in NCBI GenBank (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/genbank/) or the ICTV VMR as of the date of manuscript submission. This threshold distinguishes genuinely novel AI-generated sequences from reconstructions or minor modifications of known natural viruses. Sequence identity must be assessed by pairwise global alignment (e.g., using BLAST or equivalent) as reported in the publication. 5. Peer-Reviewed Publication: The results must appear in a peer-reviewed publication. Eligible publications are articles published in journals indexed in PubMed/MEDLINE (https://www.nlm.nih.gov/medline/medline_overview.html) that employ editorial peer review. The publication must have a final publication date (online or print) on or before December 31, 2026, 11:59 PM UTC. 6. Handling of Preprints: Preprints (e.g., on bioRxiv, medRxiv, or arXiv) that have not completed peer review and been published in a PubMed/MEDLINE-indexed journal by December 31, 2026, 11:59 PM UTC, do not count toward resolution, regardless of their content. Only publications with a confirmed peer-reviewed publication date by the deadline are eligible. 7. Monitoring Period: Only publications reporting work conducted and submitted on or after March 19, 2026 (the start date of this question) through December 31, 2026, 11:59 PM UTC are eligible. If the peer-reviewed publication date falls within this window, the question may resolve Yes regardless of when the underlying experiments were performed. 8. Resolution Source: Resolution will be determined by searching PubMed (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/) for relevant publications. The resolver should search for terms including "AI-generated virus genome," "generative model virus," "language model viral genome," "de novo virus genome design," and related queries. If a candidate publication is identified, the resolver should verify that it meets all criteria above by examining the full text. This question resolves No if no such peer-reviewed publication meeting all of the above criteria is identified by December 31, 2026, 11:59 PM UTC.

Forecast rationale

This question requires ALL of the following to be met by December 31, 2026: (1) an AI generative model produces a mammalian-infecting virus genome, (2) the genome is functional in mammalian cell culture, (3) it has <90% nucleotide identity to any known natural virus, (4) the results appear in a peer-reviewed PubMed-indexed publication, and (5) the work was submitted on or after March 19, 2026. Key factors strongly favoring NO: 1. No existing capability demonstrated: As of March 2026, no group has demonstrated this capability even in preprint form. The state of the art is functional bacteriophage genomes (King et al., 2025 preprint). The gap from phage to mammalian virus is substantial given host-pathogen complexity, immune evasion requirements, and regulatory architecture. 2. Intentional safety exclusions in leading models: Evo 2, the most capable genome-scale generative model, explicitly excluded eukaryotic viral sequences from its training data [47a997, b36600]. Its performance on human viral proteins is 'essentially random.' Any group attempting this would need to fine-tune independently on mammalian viral data. 3. Extremely tight publication timeline: The work must be submitted on or after March 19, 2026, AND published in a peer-reviewed journal by December 31, 2026 - only ~9 months. For high-impact journals, median submission-to-publication is 8-14 months. Even with fast-track review, this leaves almost no margin. The research itself (model fine-tuning, genome generation, DNA synthesis, mammalian cell culture validation, characterization) would need to be essentially complete now. 4. The conjunction problem: Meeting ALL criteria simultaneously is extremely demanding. The <90% novelty threshold combined with functional mammalian virus activity represents a frontier challenge that experts believe is 1-2+ years away. Each additional requirement (AI-generated + mammalian + functional + novel + peer-reviewed + within timeframe) multiplicatively reduces probability. 5. Regulatory and biosecurity barriers: New legislation (Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act, 2026), DNA synthesis screening protocols, institutional biosafety reviews, and journal dual-use research vetting all add substantial friction [2d2d5b]. Factors weakly favoring YES: - Some mammalian viruses (parvoviruses, ~5 kb) are comparable in size to already-designed phages - Fine-tuning of foundation models on viral data is technically feasible - AAV-related AI design work shows progress in the mammalian virus space [16cfe9] - Commercial incentives in gene therapy could motivate research However, these factors are substantially outweighed by the barriers above. The AAV capsid design work involves protein-level engineering, not whole-genome de novo generation meeting the <90% identity threshold. Key uncertainties: 1. Unknown ongoing research: Could a well-resourced group already have near-complete results ready to submit? No evidence suggests this, but absence of evidence isn't evidence of absence. If such work exists, probability increases significantly. 2. Therapeutic virus engineering edge cases: Could AAV or oncolytic virus engineering inadvertently meet all criteria? This seems unlikely given the <90% identity requirement, which is far more novel than typical therapeutic vector modifications. 3. Fast-track publication: Could a study achieve publication in <6 months? Possible for some journals but unlikely for work of this dual-use sensitivity. 4. Non-US/EU research groups: Could a group operating outside major regulatory frameworks accomplish this? Possible, but they would still need PubMed-indexed publication. Market and expert signals: No prediction markets directly address this question, but expert consensus (including Evo 2 authors, Kevin Esvelt, NTI) strongly suggests this capability is not expected within the 2026 timeframe. The Nature news feature (March 2026) explicitly notes 'significant further advances are needed.' The probability is very low, constrained primarily by the absence of any demonstrated capability, the tight submission-to-publication window, and the conjunction of demanding technical and procedural requirements.

Proto-question Stage 1

Will any major frontier AI lab (Anthropic, OpenAI, Google DeepMind, or Meta) publicly classify a model as requiring their highest-tier biosecurity safeguards (e.g., Anthropic's ASL-4 or equivalent) by December 31, 2026?

Why this question? The paper discusses how state actors might exploit advanced AI systems and notes that 'companies might need to pause deployment for over one year to implement SL 4 controls.' As of early 2026, Anthropic has activated ASL-3 safeguards for Claude 4 Opus but no model has triggered ASL-4. Whether rapidly improving AI capabilities will trigger the highest-tier biological risk safeguards is a key upstream indicator of the risk level the paper models.

Paper reference: Footnote 8 states: 'Mitigating state-level misuse of frontier models could require restricting capability improvements, enhanced monitoring and/or securing model weights as per SL 4 standards (RAND, 2024). Although the costs are uncertain, several experts we consulted suggested that companies might need to pause deployment for over one year to implement SL 4 controls.'

Background

As of March 2026, four major frontier AI labs—Anthropic, OpenAI, Google DeepMind, and Meta—maintain voluntary safety frameworks that define escalating tiers of risk for AI capabilities, including in the domain of biosecurity (i.e., the risk that an AI system could materially assist in the development or deployment of biological weapons). Each framework defines capability thresholds at which increasingly stringent safeguards must be activated: - Anthropic uses its Responsible Scaling Policy (RSP), currently at Version 3.0 (published February 24, 2026). The RSP defines AI Safety Levels (ASLs). Anthropic activated ASL-3 safeguards in May 2025 for Claude 4 Opus and subsequent models of similar or greater capability. ASL-4, which would represent the next tier of safeguards, remains "largely undefined" according to the RSP v3.0. Notably, the RSP v3.0 represents a significant overhaul: Anthropic has moved away from binary ASL threshold "tripwires" toward a system of "Frontier Safety Roadmaps" and regular "Risk Reports," and removed its binding commitment to pause scaling if safety measures fall behind. In its February 2026 Risk Report (https://anthropic.com/feb-2026-risk-report), Anthropic noted that biological risks exist in a "zone of ambiguity" where models show significant biological knowledge but evaluation science is not yet robust enough to definitively rule capabilities in or out. No model has triggered ASL-4-level biosecurity safeguards. - OpenAI uses its Preparedness Framework, currently at Version 2 (updated April 2025). It defines two tracked risk levels: High and Critical, across domains including biological and chemical threats. Under this framework, GPT-5 was classified as High (not Critical) for biological and chemical weapons risk, as disclosed in the GPT-5 System Card. A "Critical" biological risk classification would indicate the model enables development of a highly dangerous novel biological threat comparable to a novel CDC Class A biological agent. No OpenAI model has been publicly assessed as Critical for biosecurity. Note that OpenAI's updated framework includes a provision to "adjust" safeguards if rivals release high-risk systems without comparable protections. - Google DeepMind uses its Frontier Safety Framework (FSF), currently at Version 3.0 (published September 2025). The FSF defines Critical Capability Levels (CCLs) across domains including biosecurity. CCLs are tiered thresholds at which models may pose heightened risk absent additional mitigation. Biosecurity CCLs escalate from capabilities that provide limited uplift to capabilities that could enable creation of novel dangerous biological agents. No Google DeepMind model has been publicly assessed as reaching the highest defined biosecurity CCL. - Meta uses its Frontier AI Framework (published February 2025). It defines two risk categories: High and Critical, covering cybersecurity and chemical/biological weapons domains. A "Critical" risk classification for biological weapons would indicate a system capable of enabling catastrophic biological harm that cannot be adequately mitigated. No Meta model has been publicly assessed as reaching the Critical risk level for biosecurity. This question asks whether any of these labs will formally classify a model at the highest biosecurity risk tier in their framework during 2026. This is genuinely uncertain: while AI capabilities are advancing rapidly, the highest tiers at every lab represent near-catastrophic capability thresholds, and several labs have been loosening their safety commitments, creating uncertainty about whether formal triggers will be reached or even invoked. ---

Resolution criteria

This question resolves Yes if, between March 19, 2026 (00:00 UTC) and December 31, 2026 (23:59 UTC), any of the following four organizations publishes a qualifying announcement that explicitly classifies or assesses a specific AI model as having reached or exceeded the highest-tier biosecurity risk level defined in that organization's own published safety framework, as specified below.

Forecast rationale

This question asks whether any of four major AI labs will formally classify a model at their highest-tier biosecurity risk level by December 31, 2026. I synthesize the evidence as follows: Base Rate & Status Quo: The historical base rate is 0% — across approximately 6-8 major frontier model evaluations since safety frameworks were established in 2023-2024, zero models have been classified at the highest biosecurity tier. All current frontier models (GPT-5.4, Claude 4.6, Gemini 3 Pro, Llama 4) sit at the second-highest tier (High/ASL-3). The ASL-2→ASL-3 transition took ~20 months; we are only ~10 months into ASL-3 as of March 2026. Market Signal: Metaculus community forecast for this exact question is ~18% [055a22, 85585c]. The median estimate for Anthropic reaching ASL-4 is August 2027, suggesting only a ~25-30% chance by end of 2026. These are important anchors. Key Uncertainties: 1. Next-generation model releases (GPT-6, Claude 5): If released in late 2026, these could cross capability thresholds. If delayed to 2027, the probability drops significantly. Development cycles suggest this is possible but not certain within the window. 2. Evaluation science maturity: Anthropic's 'zone of ambiguity' [883f2f, a3f0dd] allows labs to remain at ASL-3/High without definitive evidence either way. If evaluation methods improve enough to produce a clear 'smoking gun,' classification becomes more likely; if they remain noisy, labs retain plausible deniability. 3. Institutional incentives vs. regulatory pressure: Labs have actively loosened frameworks (Anthropic RSP v3.0 removed binding pause commitments [649691]; OpenAI allows 'adjustment' if competitors release without protections). This creates strong incentives to avoid 'Critical' classification. However, AISI scrutiny and the International AI Safety Report create countervailing pressure. 4. Material vs. informational barriers: Even if models show impressive biological knowledge, the 'Critical' thresholds specifically require enabling catastrophic harm (e.g., novel CDC Class A agents). Physical barriers to bioweapon creation remain significant [da8087]. Arguments Favoring Yes: Agentic capabilities are advancing rapidly; the transition from 'knowing' to 'doing' biology is underway; regulatory pressure is increasing; Dario Amodei warned of 2026-2027 as a 'critical window.' Arguments Favoring No: Zero base rate; strong commercial incentives against classification; framework loosening gives labs flexibility; evaluation science gaps prevent definitive 'Critical' findings; physical/material barriers remain; labs can expand ASL-3/High definitions rather than escalate. Synthesis: The institutional barriers to a formal highest-tier classification are substantial. Labs have restructured their frameworks to provide more flexibility, and the evaluation science gap gives them room to remain at 'High/ASL-3.' The 0% historical base rate is telling. However, 287 days is meaningful, next-gen models may arrive, and external pressure is growing. The Metaculus estimate of ~18% is a reasonable anchor, but I believe the institutional resistance and evaluation gaps warrant a slightly lower estimate. The structural shift away from binary tripwires toward flexible 'Risk Reports' [649691] is particularly significant — it means labs can apply higher-tier mitigations without the formal classification that would trigger this question.

Adversarial review NEEDS_REVISION

Assessment: NEEDS_REVISION

ASSESSMENT: NEEDS_REVISION REVIEW: Several substantive issues identified: 1. Anthropic's ASL-4 being "largely undefined" creates a near-impossible resolution criterion. The question acknowledges ASL-4 is "largely undefined" and then tries to define its own resolution threshold ("capabilities that go beyond the 'zone of ambiguity'"). But the resolution criteria require Anthropic to explicitly classify a model as meeting or exceeding the highest tier. If Anthropic hasn't defined ASL-4, it can't explicitly classify a model as reaching it. This creates a catch-22: the question requires a public classification against a standard that doesn't yet exist. The question's own attempt to define what "would count" substitutes the question author's interpretation for Anthropic's framework, which contradicts the stated requirement that classification must come from the lab itself. Anthropic's RSP v3.0 deliberately left ASL-4+ undefined [676c79], and the TIME article confirms that Anthropic has moved away from binary threshold "tripwires" toward continuous risk reporting [676c79]. This makes the Anthropic resolution criterion much harder to satisfy than those for other labs, creating an asymmetry. 2. "Highest-tier" is a moving target—labs can add higher tiers and move the goalpost. The resolution criteria state: "If a lab restructures or renames its safety framework tiers...the question still applies to whatever tier is explicitly designated as the highest level." This means if any lab adds a new tier above the current "highest" (e.g., OpenAI adds a tier above "Critical"), the goalpost shifts upward. The question does not protect against this scenario. For open-ended frameworks like Google DeepMind's CCLs, where new higher levels could theoretically be defined at any time, the "highest defined biosecurity CCL" is inherently unstable—the lab could define a new CCL level that no model reaches, ensuring the question resolves No. 3. Background facts are mostly accurate but the Anthropic RSP v3.0 changes deserve stronger emphasis. The question correctly notes that RSP v3.0 removed the binding commitment to pause scaling. However, the TIME article from March 2026 [676c79] describes an even more fundamental shift: Anthropic has "radically overhauled" its RSP, moving away from binary ASL thresholds toward a system of "Frontier Safety Roadmaps" and regular "Risk Reports." If Anthropic's framework is evolving away from explicit ASL-tier classification altogether, the resolution criterion for Anthropic may become structurally impossible to satisfy—not because capabilities haven't advanced, but because Anthropic's disclosure model no longer works in a way that would produce a "qualifying announcement." 4. The question is likely to resolve trivially NO, limiting forecasting value. No lab has come close to the highest tier. GPT-5 was classified as "High" (not Critical) for bio/chem risk. No DeepMind model has reached highest CCL. Given the extremely high bars set for these tiers (e.g., enabling development of novel CDC Class A biological agents), the probability of any lab publicly declaring a model has reached these thresholds in the next ~9 months is very low. The question would benefit from either (a) a longer time horizon, or (b) asking about a lower but still meaningful tier (e.g., any model classified as "High" or above across all four labs, or asking about the second-highest tier). 5. Publishing practices: Labs do publish risk tier classifications, so this aspect is sound. OpenAI published GPT-5's "High" biological risk classification in its system card. Anthropic publishes Risk Reports. Google DeepMind publishes safety evaluations with CCL assessments. So the resolution source mechanism is viable—labs do publicly disclose risk tier assessments. Fix the Anthropic criterion: Either (a) remove Anthropic from the question and limit to OpenAI/DeepMind/Meta which have clearly defined terminal tiers, or (b) define the Anthropic resolution criterion as "Anthropic defines and then classifies a model as meeting ASL-4 or higher for biosecurity," making explicit that both definition and classification must occur. 2. Address the goalpost-moving problem: Add language stating that the "highest tier" is frozen as of the question start date (March 19, 2026). If a lab adds new tiers above the current highest, the resolution criteria should still reference the tier that was highest as of the start date. 3. Consider lowering the bar or extending the time horizon: Given that the highest tier at every lab represents near-apocalyptic capability, consider either (a) extending the resolution date to December 31, 2028, or (b) reframing the question around a lower but still significant threshold—e.g., "Will any lab publicly classify a model at or above 'High' risk for biosecurity?" or "Will any lab classify a model at the second-highest biosecurity tier?" 4. Alternative reformulation: A more forecasting-interesting question might be: "Will any major frontier AI lab publicly classify a model as exceeding its current biosecurity risk classification before December 31, 2027?" This captures the genuine uncertainty (will risk levels escalate?) without depending on poorly-defined terminal tiers.

Decomposition Stage 6b 5 subquestions
Initial: 14%→Research-informed: 5%
SQ1: How rapidly are frontier AI models' biosecurity-relevant capabilities advancing, and how close are current models (as of early 2026) to the specific capability thresholds that would trigger highest-tier biosecurity classifications at any of the four major labs?
As of March 2026, frontier AI models' biosecurity-relevant capabilities are advancing rapidly but have not yet crossed the highest-tier thresholds defined by any of the four major labs. The UK AI Security Institute's December 2025 Frontier AI Trends Report found that models already exceed PhD-level experts by up to 60% on open-ended biology questions and up to 90% on troubleshooting wet-lab experiments [9c3f35]. The 2026 International AI Safety Report (published February 3, 2026) confirmed that a recent model outperformed 94% of domain experts at troubleshooting virology laboratory protocols [e92596]. Despite this, every lab that has publicly reported evaluations has classified its latest models below the highest tier: Anthropic (ASL-4 / CBRN-4): Claude Opus 4 (May 2025) was deemed "significantly far away" from ASL-4 thresholds [f21951]. Claude Opus 4.6 (February 2026) was also determined not to cross the CBRN-4 threshold, but Anthropic acknowledged that "confidently ruling out these thresholds is becoming increasingly difficult" and described the CBRN-4 rule-out as involving "substantial uncertainty" [7fc28f]. OpenAI (Critical): All GPT-5 series models (GPT-5, GPT-5.2, GPT-5.3-Codex, GPT-5.4 Thinking, released between late 2025 and March 2026) have been classified as "High" — one tier below "Critical" — in the Biological and Chemical domain under their Preparedness Framework. No model has reached "Critical." Google DeepMind (highest CCL): Gemini 3 was evaluated against Critical Capability Levels (CCLs) for biosecurity as part of the Frontier Safety Framework 3.0. Google's February 2026 AI Responsibility Report confirmed comprehensive evaluations were conducted but did not disclose specific biological risk scores [808a24]. No public statement indicates any Gemini model has triggered the highest biosecurity CCL. Meta (Critical): Meta's Frontier AI Framework uses Moderate/High/Critical tiers for biological risk. Limited public reporting on specific bio evaluation results is available, but no public disclosure suggests any Meta model has been classified at the Critical level. The trajectory from March–December 2026 suggests continued rapid improvement. AISI found capabilities doubling roughly every eight months [9c3f35]. Anthropic's own assessment shows the gap between current capabilities and ASL-4 is narrowing measurably between model generations [f21951][7fc28f]. However, the highest-tier thresholds (ASL-4, Critical, highest CCL) are deliberately set very high — requiring capabilities like meaningful uplift for experts to create novel dangerous biological agents or enabling development of novel CDC Class A agents — and current models still fall short on end-to-end tasks such as novel pathogen design and synthesis planning, even as they excel at component tasks like protocol generation and troubleshooting.
SQ2: How mature and reliable is the current evaluation science for detecting dangerous biosecurity capabilities in AI models, and does the persistent 'zone of ambiguity' make it unlikely that any lab could confidently trigger a highest-tier biosecurity classification by end of 2026?
The current evaluation science for detecting dangerous biosecurity capabilities in AI models is characterized by a significant and well-documented "zone of ambiguity" that makes confidently distinguishing between High and Critical (or ASL-3 and ASL-4) risk levels extremely difficult. As of early 2026, multiple frontier labs have acknowledged this challenge: Anthropic's Claude Opus 4.6 System Card (February 2026) explicitly states that "confidently ruling out these thresholds is becoming increasingly difficult" due to benchmark saturation, epistemic uncertainty, measurement limitations, and limited understanding of threat actors [cfbcf4]. The International AI Safety Report 2026 (February 2026) identifies a fundamental "evaluation gap" where standardized benchmarks fail to predict real-world risk, and real-world uplift studies are constrained by legal and treaty obligations [c595a7]. Current methodologies include automated knowledge evaluations, agentic task-based assessments, expert red-teaming, and uplift trials, but these face critical gaps: (1) difficulty testing wet-lab skills due to legal/ethical constraints of the Biological Weapons Convention, forcing reliance on benign proxy tasks; (2) inability to assess tacit knowledge transfer—the practical, embodied know-how needed for actual bioweapons work; and (3) challenges in measuring uplift over freely available baseline information. A landmark wet-lab RCT (June–August 2025, n=153, published February 2026 by METR-affiliated researchers) found that while LLMs uplift novice performance on computational biology tasks, their effect in physical laboratories remains modest and inconsistent, underscoring the gap between benchmark performance and real-world capability. Key ongoing improvement efforts include: AISI (UK AI Safety Institute) conducting end-to-end biosecurity red-teaming with OpenAI and Anthropic through 2025, revealing dozens of vulnerabilities; METR running real-world biological RCTs and developing evaluation infrastructure; the STREAM (ChemBio) standard (August 2025) establishing transparent reporting requirements for biosecurity evaluations; Epoch AI's critical June 2025 analysis showing publicly described benchmarks saturate quickly and fail to capture weapons-relevant capabilities; and the Frontier Model Forum's biosafety threshold work. Despite these gaps, the evaluation uncertainty does not straightforwardly prevent labs from acting. Labs have adopted precautionary approaches—OpenAI classified GPT-5 and subsequent models as "High" biological risk (January 2025 onward), and Anthropic activated ASL-3 protections for Claude Opus 4 (May 2025) and continued them for Claude Opus 4.6 (February 2026)—based on the inability to rule out risk rather than definitive proof of crossing critical thresholds. However, the same ambiguity makes triggering the highest tier (Critical/ASL-4) particularly challenging: Anthropic notes the margin for future rule-outs is "narrowing" but has not yet triggered ASL-4 [cfbcf4]. The evaluation gap creates a structural asymmetry where precautionary action at lower tiers is easier than definitive classification at the highest tier, potentially delaying formal highest-tier classifications even as capabilities advance rapidly.
SQ3: What are the strategic incentives and disincentives for frontier AI labs to publicly classify a model at the highest biosecurity risk tier, given the recent trend of loosening safety commitments and the competitive dynamics among labs?
The strategic incentives facing frontier AI labs strongly favor avoiding publicly classifying a model at the highest biosecurity risk tier. Five interlocking dynamics drive this conclusion: 1. Structural loosening of safety commitments (2025-2026). Anthropic's RSP v3.0, announced on February 24, 2026, removed the binding commitment to "pause the scaling and/or delay the deployment of new models" when safety measures fall behind capabilities [b7a08a]. The new policy replaces binary "bright red lines" that would trigger automatic halts with more flexible "Frontier Safety Roadmaps" and periodic "Risk Reports" published every 3-6 months [b2a35b]. Anthropic explicitly stated that ASL-4 and beyond are "beyond the containment capabilities of any single company," framing unilateral action as futile [b7a08a]. Meanwhile, OpenAI's Preparedness Framework v2 (April 2025) includes a competitive adjustment clause: "If another frontier AI developer releases a high-risk system without comparable safeguards, we may adjust our requirements." This creates a structural off-ramp from the highest-tier classification for both labs. 2. Massive commercial and reputational costs of triggering the highest tier. A "Critical" classification would, under most frameworks, require halting deployment or severely restricting access. No lab has ever triggered its highest tier. For Anthropic (ASL-5), OpenAI (Critical), Google DeepMind (highest CCL), or Meta, doing so would cede market share to rivals, alarm investors during a period of massive capital raises, and potentially invite regulatory action. Anthropic's Jared Kaplan explicitly argued that pausing while "rivals are blazing ahead" does not benefit anyone [b7a08a]. 3. Competitive dynamics create a prisoner's dilemma. If one lab classifies a model at the highest tier and restricts deployment, rivals gain a direct commercial advantage by deploying comparable models without such classification. OpenAI's adjustment clause explicitly addresses this: it creates a mechanism to lower safeguards if competitors release high-risk systems, rather than a mechanism to raise them. This creates a race-to-the-bottom dynamic where each lab has incentives to classify just below the highest tier. 4. Flexibility in evaluation methodology provides discretion. Labs design their own evaluation benchmarks, define their own thresholds, and interpret results internally before publishing. The shift from binding commitments to "living documents" (Anthropic) and adjustable frameworks (OpenAI) gives labs significant discretion in how they classify models. As of early 2026, models are classified at intermediate tiers: Anthropic at ASL-3 (since May 2025), OpenAI's GPT-5 classified as "high capability" (not Critical) in biosecurity, and Google DeepMind's Gemini 3 not crossing any Critical Capability Levels. 5. Countervailing pressures exist but are weaker. Signaling safety maturity could build trust with regulators and the public. External pressure from organizations like METR, the UK AI Security Institute, and the Frontier Model Forum pushes for transparency. The International AI Safety Report 2026 and California's AI policy report increase scrutiny. However, these pressures have not yet proven strong enough to override commercial incentives—as evidenced by the loosening trend across all labs throughout 2025-2026. Net assessment for forecasters: The institutional incentive structures strongly favor labs classifying models at intermediate tiers (e.g., "High" or ASL-3) rather than triggering their highest tier. The removal of binding pause commitments, the introduction of competitive adjustment clauses, the commercial costs of restricting deployment, and the discretion labs have in evaluation methodology all point in the same direction. The most likely path to a highest-tier classification would require either (a) external evaluators publicly demonstrating capabilities that make it impossible for a lab to deny, (b) a regulatory mandate, or (c) a catastrophic incident that forces reclassification. Absent these forcing functions, the incentive environment as of March 2026 makes voluntary highest-tier classification unlikely.
SQ4: What is the likelihood that any of the four labs will revise their safety frameworks before end of 2026 in ways that raise, redefine, or otherwise move the goalposts on their highest-tier biosecurity thresholds, making formal classification less likely?
Summary: Likelihood of Framework Revisions That Raise Biosecurity Thresholds (March–December 2026) All four major frontier AI labs (Anthropic, OpenAI, Google DeepMind, Meta) have established a clear pattern of revising their safety frameworks, with each revision generally adding flexibility, simplifying structures, or redefining thresholds. This pattern makes it moderately to highly likely that further revisions will occur before end of 2026. Here are the key findings: Anthropic: Has undergone 5 RSP versions (v1.0 in Sep 2023 → v3.0 on Feb 24, 2026). The most recent RSP v3.0 removed the unconditional commitment to pause development if dangerous capabilities are found, replacing it with a more flexible approach. Critically, ASL-4 remains "largely undefined" as of RSP v3.0 — Anthropic intentionally left it undefined. Their Frontier Safety Roadmap (published Feb 2026) commits to selecting 1–3 safety projects by April 1, 2026, which may include ASL-4 definition work. ASL-4 is likely to be formally defined sometime in 2026, but Anthropic has signaled it will set a high bar — they explicitly stated they could have "defined ASL-4 and ASL-5 safeguards in ways that made compliance easy to achieve—but this would undermine the purpose." Meanwhile, their Feb 2026 Risk Report notes automated evaluations can no longer rule out ASL-4 level autonomy, suggesting models are approaching the threshold even before it is formally defined. OpenAI: Updated from Preparedness Framework Beta (Dec 2023) to v2 (April 15, 2025). Key changes: removed Low and Medium risk levels entirely (they "were not operationally involved"), narrowed CBRN to biological and chemical only (removing nuclear/radiological), removed manipulation/persuasion as a tracked risk category, and added AI Self-improvement and Long-range Autonomy as new tracked categories. The "Critical" tier remains the highest, defined as capability that "could introduce unprecedented new pathways to severe harm." These changes broadly added flexibility and reduced the number of categories that could trigger a classification. Another revision is plausible before end of 2026 given the ~16-month gap between v1 and v2. Google DeepMind: Updated through FSF v1 (May 2024), v2 (Feb 2025), and v3 (Sep 22, 2025) — roughly every 6–8 months. The v3 update shifted methodology from assessing "model capability in isolation" to assessing "additional expected harm that may result in the real world," which is arguably a higher bar [4bdbc1]. The biosecurity CCL ("CBRN Uplift Level 1") requires showing that a model "provides low to medium resourced actors uplift in reference scenarios resulting in additional expected harm at severe scale" [4bdbc1]. No model has triggered a CCL to date [4bdbc1]. Given the ~6-month revision cycle, a v4 update is plausible by mid-2026. Meta: Published its Frontier AI Framework in February 2025, with two tiers (High-risk and Critical-risk) focused on cybersecurity and chemical/biological weapons. No known revisions as of March 2026. Critical risk is defined as models "too dangerous to develop." Given that other labs revise every 6–12 months, an update from Meta is plausible but uncertain in 2026. Regulatory Constraints: External constraints are mostly weak. The Seoul AI Safety Commitments (May 2024) are voluntary and require labs to set thresholds but do not specify what those thresholds must be. The EU AI Act's GPAI Code of Practice requires safety frameworks and risk assessments for models above 10^25 FLOPs, with enforcement beginning August 2026, but does not dictate specific internal threshold levels. The Trump administration revoked Biden's AI safety EO in January 2025 and has actively deregulated, imposing no federal constraints on labs' internal frameworks. California's SB 53 (signed Sep 29, 2025) requires transparency about safety frameworks but does not dictate threshold levels. In sum, no current regulation prevents labs from raising their own thresholds. Overall Assessment: The likelihood is moderate-to-high that at least one lab will revise its framework before end of 2026 in ways that add flexibility. Anthropic is the most consequential case: ASL-4 will likely be formally defined in 2026, and when it is, the definition will likely set a deliberately high bar. OpenAI and DeepMind are on revision cycles that make updates plausible. Regulatory constraints are too weak to prevent threshold revisions. The net effect is that framework revisions could make formal highest-tier classification somewhat less likely by raising or redefining what triggers such classification.
SQ5: What role could external pressure—from independent evaluators (UK AISI, METR), regulators, leaked evaluations, or public incidents—play in forcing or motivating a lab to publicly classify a model at the highest biosecurity tier by end of 2026?
External Pressures on Frontier AI Labs Regarding Highest-Tier Biosecurity Classification (March 19 – December 31, 2026) Five distinct sources of external pressure could force or motivate a frontier AI lab to publicly classify a model at the highest biosecurity tier by end of 2026: 1. Independent Evaluators (UK AISI, METR): The UK AI Security Institute (AISI) published its first Frontier AI Trends Report in December 2025, synthesizing two years of evaluations across 30+ frontier models, finding that AI models have "far surpassed PhD-level expertise in chemistry and biology" and that self-replication success rates went from 5% to 60% between 2023 and 2025. The International AI Safety Report 2026, published February 2026, found that frontier models can outperform 94% of domain experts at troubleshooting virology lab protocols [349e31]. METR maintains an ongoing index of frontier safety policies (as of December 2025, covering 12 companies) and conducts independent capability evaluations [e51f3e]. If either AISI or METR publicly identifies capabilities that a lab's own framework would classify as highest-tier, the lab faces significant reputational and political pressure to acknowledge it. This pressure pathway is already active and growing in force. 2. Regulatory Bodies (EU AI Act, California SB 53): Two major regulatory deadlines fall within the March–December 2026 window: - California SB 53 (effective January 1, 2026): Requires frontier AI developers to implement safety protocols addressing catastrophic risk, publish transparency reports, report critical safety incidents to the California Office of Emergency Services within 15 days (24 hours if ongoing), and maintain whistleblower protections. This law is already in force during the forecast window. - EU AI Act – GPAI Enforcement (August 2, 2026): The European AI Office begins enforcement of all obligations for general-purpose AI model providers, including those with systemic risk. Providers of GPAI models with systemic risk must conduct model evaluations, assess and mitigate systemic risks, report serious incidents, and ensure cybersecurity protections. Non-compliance can result in fines. - New York RAISE Act (effective July 1, 2027): While outside the immediate window, its passage in early 2026 signals growing state-level regulatory momentum and requires faster incident reporting (72 hours vs. SB 53's 15 days). 3. Whistleblowers/Leaks: The AI whistleblower ecosystem is maturing rapidly. In July 2024, OpenAI whistleblowers filed a disclosure with the SEC alleging the company barred staff from airing safety risks. OpenAI subsequently became the only major lab to publish its full whistleblowing policy, though only after media pressure [97f06f]. California SB 53 now provides explicit legal protections for employees involved in risk assessment. The AI Whistleblower Initiative (AIWI) published an assessment of OpenAI's policy in January 2026. A "digital safe" platform (PSST) for international AI whistleblowers has also emerged. If internal evaluations show highest-tier capabilities that are not publicly disclosed, the legal and institutional infrastructure for whistleblowing is now substantially stronger than in prior years. 4. Biosecurity Incidents: As of early 2026, no publicly known AI-enabled biosecurity incident has occurred, but the landscape is shifting rapidly. The International AI Safety Report 2026 documents that labs have already triggered elevated internal alerts: OpenAI designated GPT-5-Thinking and ChatGPT-Agent as "High capability" under their Preparedness Framework; Anthropic released Claude Opus 4 at ASL-3; Google DeepMind's Gemini 2.5 Deep Think triggered a "Critical Capability Levels" early warning alert for chemical/biological risk [349e31]. A RAND-NTI workshop at the 2025 AI Action Summit specifically addressed AI-biotech biosecurity governance. A near-miss or demonstration event during the forecast window—even by independent researchers—could create intense public and political pressure for labs to formally assess and disclose their models' full biosecurity capabilities. 5. Civil Society Organizations: The Future of Life Institute's AI Safety Index (Summer 2025, published July 17, 2025) found the industry is "fundamentally unprepared" for its own stated AGI goals, with only Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google DeepMind conducting substantive dangerous-capability testing [97f06f]. It graded all labs poorly (best: Anthropic at C+; Meta at D). Civil society organizations like the Council on Strategic Risks publish ongoing AIxBio analyses, and Stanford Law has published gap analyses of SB 53. These organizations create reputational pressure through public grading, advocacy, and media engagement, making it harder for labs to quietly avoid highest-tier classifications if evidence warrants them. Overall Assessment: The external pressure environment in the March–December 2026 window is substantially stronger than in any prior period. The combination of (a) legally binding disclosure requirements now in force (SB 53 since January 2026, EU AI Act enforcement from August 2026), (b) increasingly capable independent evaluators publishing findings that can contradict lab self-assessments, (c) strengthened whistleblower infrastructure and legal protections, (d) rapidly advancing model capabilities approaching biosecurity thresholds, and (e) active civil society monitoring creates a realistic pathway by which external forces could compel a lab to publicly classify a model at the highest biosecurity tier—even if the lab might otherwise prefer not to do so.

ITNSSS31

Proto-question Stage 1

Will the U.S. Congress pass federal legislation mandating universal DNA synthesis screening (covering non-federally-funded orders) by December 31, 2026?

Why this question? The paper discusses AI-enabled pathogen design as a key risk pathway and notes that DNA synthesis screening is a critical mitigation. The current OSTP Framework only covers federally funded research. The Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026 (S. 3741), introduced by Senators Cotton (R-AR) and Klobuchar (D-MN) on January 29, 2026, would extend screening requirements to all orders. Whether Congress passes this or similar legislation is genuinely uncertain — it has bipartisan support but Congressional timelines are unpredictable.

Paper reference: The paper notes that 'improved real-time information sharing and screening of dual-use materials may increase the economic and political costs of bioweapon development' (Section 3, bullet on offense-defense balance). Table 9 references scenarios where AI helps design pathogens that bypass detection. The paper also discusses DNA synthesis screening evasion (citing Wittmann et al. 2025).

Background

Nucleic acid synthesis screening is a biosecurity measure in which providers of synthetic DNA and RNA check incoming orders against databases of known pathogen sequences to prevent malicious actors from obtaining dangerous biological materials. The current U.S. screening regime is governed by the "Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening," released by the White House Office of Science and Technology Policy (OSTP) in September 2024 (https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf). This Framework defines key terms including "Sequence of Concern" (broadly, a nucleotide sequence that is a best match to a sequence from a federally regulated pathogen or toxin) and establishes requirements for sequence-level and customer-level screening. Critically, the 2024 OSTP Framework applies only as a condition of receiving U.S. governmental life sciences research funding — it does not mandate screening for privately funded or commercial orders. On May 5, 2025, President Trump signed Executive Order 14292, "Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research" (https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/05/08/2025-08266/improving-the-safety-and-security-of-biological-research), which directed OSTP to develop a strategy that "shall include actions to achieve comprehensive, scalable, and verifiable nucleic acid synthesis screening in non-federally funded" contexts. However, this executive order does not itself create a legal mandate for universal screening — it only directs the development of a strategy. On January 29, 2026, Senators Tom Cotton (R-AR) and Amy Klobuchar (D-MN) introduced S. 3741, the "Biosecurity Modernization and Innovation Act of 2026" (https://www.congress.gov/bill/119th-congress/senate-bill/3741/all-info). The bill would require the Secretary of Commerce to promulgate regulations requiring all nucleic acid synthesis providers to screen orders — not just those involving federal funding. The bill was read twice and referred to the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science, and Transportation on January 29, 2026. As of March 19, 2026, no further committee action (hearing, markup, or vote) has been reported. The bill has bipartisan sponsorship and endorsements from organizations including the Nuclear Threat Initiative (NTI), the Federation of American Scientists (FAS), and the Johns Hopkins Center for Health Security. No companion bill has been identified in the House of Representatives. The FY2026 National Defense Authorization Act (NDAA), signed into law on December 18, 2025, included the BIOSECURE Act (restricting federal procurement from certain biotechnology companies of concern) but did not include a universal DNA synthesis screening mandate. Historically, most bills introduced in Congress do not advance beyond committee referral. However, S. 3741 has notable bipartisan support, alignment with the executive branch's stated goals in EO 14292, and endorsements from prominent biosecurity organizations. The 119th Congress (2025–2026) expires in January 2027. ---

Resolution criteria

This question resolves Yes if, between March 19, 2026 (00:00 UTC) and December 31, 2026 (23:59 UTC), the President of the United States signs into law any federal legislation that includes a legally binding requirement for nucleic acid synthesis providers operating in the United States to screen synthesis orders against sequences of concern, where this requirement applies to all orders — not solely those funded by U.S. government research funding. Specific clarifications: 1. Definition of "pass": The legislation must be signed into law by the President, or enacted through a congressional override of a presidential veto. Mere passage by one or both chambers of Congress does not suffice. 2. Definition of "DNA synthesis screening": For purposes of this question, "nucleic acid synthesis screening" refers to the practice of checking orders for synthetic nucleic acids (DNA or RNA) against databases of sequences associated with pathogens, toxins, or other biological agents of concern, and conducting follow-up screening (including customer verification) when a potential match is identified, consistent with the definition in the 2024 OSTP Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening (https://aspr.hhs.gov/S3/Documents/OSTP-Nucleic-Acid-Synthesis-Screening-Framework-Sep2024.pdf). 3. "Universal" requirement: The mandate must apply to all commercial nucleic acid synthesis providers operating in the United States (i.e., it is not limited to providers serving federally funded customers or to federally funded orders only). It is sufficient if the law requires all providers to screen all orders; it is not necessary that the law separately mandate obligations on purchasers. 4. Broader legislation: The requirement need not be enacted as a standalone bill. If the relevant DNA synthesis screening mandate is included as a provision within a broader bill (e.g., an omnibus spending bill, a National Defense Authorization Act, or any other legislative vehicle), that satisfies the resolution criteria, provided the signed law contains a legally binding universal screening requirement as defined above. 5. Regulatory delegation: If the signed legislation directs a federal agency (e.g., the Department of Commerce) to promulgate regulations establishing universal screening, this counts — even if the implementing regulations have not yet been finalized by December 31, 2026 — as long as the statute itself creates a binding legal mandate for universal screening (not merely a directive to "study" or "develop a strategy" for screening).

Forecast rationale

This question asks whether the U.S. President will sign into law legislation mandating universal nucleic acid synthesis screening (covering all orders, not just federally funded ones) by December 31, 2026. Here is my analysis: Current State: S. 3741, introduced January 29, 2026, by Senators Cotton and Klobuchar, is the primary legislative vehicle. As of March 19, 2026, it has been referred to the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science, and Transportation with no hearings, markups, or further action reported [3539a3]. Crucially, no House companion bill exists. Base Rates Against: Historical base rates are strongly unfavorable. Only 1-4% of introduced bills become law. ~80-90% of bills referred to committee never receive a hearing. Bills introduced in the second year of a Congress in an election year face even worse odds. The absence of a House companion is a major structural bottleneck. Favorable Factors: Bipartisan sponsorship (Cotton-Klobuchar), alignment with EO 14292, institutional endorsements from NTI, FAS, and Johns Hopkins, industry support from major players wanting a level playing field [a04311], and the BIOSECURE Act precedent showing biosecurity provisions can ride must-pass bills like the NDAA [2a07a1]. Critical Negative Signal: The FY2026 NDAA (signed December 18, 2025) included the BIOSECURE Act but did NOT include a universal screening mandate, even though the concept was already being discussed. This suggests it was not considered a 'must-pass' priority for that cycle. Most Plausible Pathway: Attachment to the FY2027 NDAA or year-end omnibus. However, this requires: (1) committee action on S. 3741 or equivalent language being drafted, (2) a House process or companion, (3) surviving conference negotiations, and (4) presidential signature. The election year compresses the legislative calendar significantly. Market Signal: Manifold Markets reportedly estimates ~40% for 'Will the U.S. mandate DNA synthesis screening for all providers by 2027?' This is essentially the same timeframe. However, I believe this estimate is too high given the concrete structural obstacles—no committee action after nearly two months, no House companion, election year dynamics, and the precedent of the FY2026 NDAA excluding this provision. Key Uncertainties: 1. Will S. 3741 receive committee action? If hearings/markup occur by summer 2026, probability increases significantly (perhaps to 20-25%). If it remains stalled through September, probability drops to ~3-5%. 2. Will a House companion bill emerge? Without one, the bill must be attached to a larger vehicle. The emergence of a House companion would be a strong positive signal. 3. Will the NDAA or omnibus serve as a vehicle? If leadership prioritizes this for the FY2027 NDAA, the probability rises substantially. If it's excluded (as it was from FY2026 NDAA), standalone passage is extremely unlikely. 4. Will an external trigger (biosecurity incident, AI-bio threat) create urgency? A catalyzing event could dramatically accelerate the timeline. Synthesis: Blending the low base rates (~2-5%), the favorable but insufficient political dynamics, the concrete structural obstacles, and partially weighting the ~40% Manifold estimate (which I believe overestimates the probability given current legislative stagnation), I arrive at approximately 10%.

Adversarial review PASS

Assessment: PASS

ASSESSMENT: PASS REVIEW: This is a well-constructed forecasting question with accurate background facts, clear resolution criteria, and meaningful uncertainty. Key findings from research: 1. Background facts are accurate: S. 3741 was indeed introduced on January 29, 2026, by Senators Cotton and Klobuchar, referred to the Senate Commerce Committee, and has had no further committee action as of March 19, 2026. The description of EO 14292, the 2024 OSTP Framework, and the FY2026 NDAA's BIOSECURE Act (without universal screening) are all verified. 2. Resolution criteria are well-specified: The question properly handles key edge cases — regulatory delegation (point 5), inclusion in broader legislation (point 4), and the distinction between a mandate and a mere "study" directive. These are the exact edge cases that would matter for this topic. 3. The question captures genuine uncertainty: While the base rate for bills dying in committee is high (~95%), there are plausible paths to YES: (a) inclusion as a provision in the FY2027 NDAA (the FY2026 NDAA was signed Dec 18, 2025, so FY2027 NDAA could be signed in Dec 2026); (b) attachment to omnibus spending legislation; (c) accelerated standalone passage due to bipartisan sponsorship and alignment with EO 14292. The probability is likely low (5-15%) but not trivially zero, making it informative for forecasters. 4. One nuance worth noting but not disqualifying: The FAS analysis [796bf1] describes S. 3741 as giving the Administration 90 days to assess and design biosecurity oversight, suggesting it may be more of a framework-directive than a direct operational mandate. However, the bill's own summary (from congress.gov and press releases) states it would "require the Secretary of Commerce to promulgate regulations requiring all nucleic acid synthesis providers to screen orders," and the resolution criteria explicitly address this scenario in point #5 (regulatory delegation counts if the statute creates a binding mandate). 5. No companion House bill is correctly noted. H.R. 3029 (Nucleic Acid Standards for Biosecurity Act) is a related but distinct bill focused on NIST developing standards rather than mandating universal screening. HR 4729 exists to codify EO 14292 but that EO itself only directs strategy development, not a binding mandate. 6. The time horizon (March-December 2026) is appropriate for the legislative dynamics involved — tight enough to be interesting but allowing for the NDAA or omnibus vehicle in late 2026.
